The&#x20;**&#x20;history of the Philippines** dates from the earliest [hominin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominini) activity in the archipelago at least by 709,000 years ago.[^cite_note-\:0-1] *[Homo luzonensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_luzonensis)*, a species of archaic humans, was present on the island of [Luzon](69375.md)[^cite_note-\:5-2][^cite_note-\:2-3] at least by 134,000 years ago.[^cite_note-\:13-4] The earliest known anatomically modern human was from [Tabon Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabon_Caves) in [Palawan](240395.md) dating about 47,000 years.[^cite_note-\:3-5] [Negrito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negrito) groups were the first inhabitants to settle in the prehistoric Philippines.[^cite_note-Reid2007-6] These were followed by [Austroasiatics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austroasiatic_language), [Papuans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_people_of_New_Guinea), and Austronesians.[^cite_note-Larena-7] By around 3000 BCE, seafaring Austronesians, who form the majority of the current population, migrated southward from Taiwan.[^cite_note-\:6-8]

Scholars generally believe that these ethnic and social groups eventually developed into various settlements or [polities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity) with varying degrees of [economic specialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_specialization), [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification), and [political organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_organization).[^cite_note-9] Some of these settlements (mostly those located on major river deltas) achieved such a scale of social complexity that some scholars believe they should be considered early [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_\(polity\)).[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] This includes the predecessors of modern-day population centers such as [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Maynila), [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)), [Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan_\(historical_polity\)), [Cebu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Cebu), [Panay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madja-as), [Bohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo-ol), [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan), [Cotabato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Maguindanao), [Lanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_Lanao), [Zamboanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanmalan), and [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)[^cite_note-Junker1998-11] as well as some [polities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity), such as [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i), whose possible location is either Mindoro or Laguna.[^cite_note-\:11-12] These polities were influenced by [Islamic](6037917.md), [Indian](14533.md), and [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_proper) cultures. Islam arrived from [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia), while Indian [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu)-[Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist)[^cite_note-SoulBook1991-13] [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Southeast_Asia), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indosphere), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_literature#In_archaic_and_ancient_Indian_languages), and [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_philosophy) arrived from the Indian subcontinent.[^cite_note-14] Some polities were [Sinified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinicization) tributary states allied to [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_proper). These small maritime states flourished from the 1st millennium.[^cite_note-\:7-15][^cite_note-16] These kingdoms traded with what are now called China, India, Japan, Thailand, Vietnam, and Indonesia. The remainder of the settlements were independent [barangays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barangay) allied with one of the larger states. These small states alternated from being part of or being influenced by larger Asian empires such as the [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty), [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit), and [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Brunei) or rebelling and waging war against them.[^cite_note-Abinales\&Amoroso20052-17]

The first recorded visit by Europeans is [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan)'s expedition, which landed in [Homonhon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homonhon) Island, now part of Guiuan, Eastern Samar, on March 16, 1521. They lost a battle against the army of [Lapulapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapulapu), chief of [Mactan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mactan), where Magellan was killed.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-\:4-19][^cite_note-20] The [Spanish Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Philippines) began with the [Pacific expansion of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain#Pacific_expansion_and_the_Philippine_trade) and the arrival of [Miguel López de Legazpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_L%C3%B3pez_de_Legazpi)'s expedition on February 13, 1565, from Mexico. He established the first permanent settlement in [Cebu](160278.md).[^cite_note-21] Much of the archipelago came under Spanish rule, creating the first unified political structure known as the [Philippines](23440.md), named after [King Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain). Spanish colonial rule saw the introduction of Christianity, the [code of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_law), and the [oldest modern university in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas). The Philippines was ruled under the Mexico-based [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Spain). After this, the colony was directly governed by Spain, following Mexico's independence.

Spanish rule ended in 1898 with Spain's defeat in the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War). The Philippines then became a territory of the United States. U.S. forces suppressed a [revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution) led by [Emilio Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Aguinaldo). [The United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_United_States) established the [Insular Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Government) to rule the Philippines. In 1907, the elected [Philippine Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Assembly) was set up with popular elections. The U.S. promised independence in the [Jones Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Law_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-Zaide-22] The [Philippine Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Commonwealth) was established in 1935, as a 10-year interim step prior to full independence. However, in 1942 during World War II, [Japan occupied the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines). The U.S. military overpowered the Japanese in 1945. The [Treaty of Manila in 1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Manila_\(1946\)) established the independent Philippine Republic.

## Timeline

Main article: [Timeline of Philippine history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Philippine_history)



## Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistory of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_the_Philippines)



Docking station and entrance to the [Tabon Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabon_Cave) Complex Site in [Palawan](240395.md), where one of the oldest human remains was located.

Stone tools and fossils of butchered animal remains discovered in Rizal, Kalinga are evidence of human presence as early as 709,000 years ago.[^cite_note-\:0-1] Researchers found 57 stone tools near rhinoceros bones bearing cut marks and some bones smashed open, suggesting that the early humans consumed the nutrient-rich marrow.[^cite_note-23] A 2023 study dated the age of fossilized remains of *Homo luzonensis* of Cagayan at about 134,000 years.[^cite_note-\:13-4]

This and the [Angono Petroglyphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angono_Petroglyphs) in [Rizal](232458.md) suggest the presence of human settlement before the arrival of the [Negritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negrito) and the [Austronesian-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_people).[^cite_note-Jett2017-24][^cite_note-Detroit2013-25] The Callao Man remains and 12 bones of three hominin individuals found by subsequent excavations in Callao Cave were later identified to belong in a new species named *[Homo luzonensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_luzonensis)*.[^cite_note-\:2-3] For [modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humans), the Tabon Man remains are the still oldest known at about 47,000 years.[^cite_note-\:3-5]



[A Developed Prehistoric Pottery Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_symbolism_in_archaeology#Sa-huynh-Kalanay_pottery_and_Lapita_pottery) existed at the Philippines from as early as 500 BCE.&#x20;

&#x20;The [Negritos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negrito) were early settlers,[^cite_note-Reid2007-6] but their appearance in the Philippines has not been reliably dated.[^cite_note-26] They were followed by speakers of the [Malayo-Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayo-Polynesian_languages), a branch of the [Austronesian language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages). The first Austronesians reached the Philippines at 3000–2200 BCE, settling the [Batanes Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batanes_Islands) and [northern Luzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Luzon).

From there, they rapidly spread downwards to the rest of the islands of the Philippines and [Southeast Asia](28741.md), as well as voyaging further east to reach the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md) by around 1500 BCE.[^cite_note-\:6-8][^cite_note-Chambers2013-27][^cite_note-mijares2006-28][^cite_note-autogenerated213-29] They assimilated the earlier [Australo-Melanesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australo-Melanesian) Negritos, resulting in the modern [Filipino ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_ethnic_groups) that all display various ratios of [genetic admixture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_admixture) between Austronesian and Negrito groups.[^cite_note-Lipson2014-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott198452-31] Before the expansion out of Taiwan, archaeological, linguistic, and genetic evidence had linked Austronesian speakers in Insular Southeast Asia to cultures such as the [Hemudu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemudu_culture) in Neolithic China, its successor the [Liangzhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liangzhu_culture),[^cite_note-autogenerated213-29][^cite_note-32] and the [Dapenkeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dapenkeng_culture) in Neolithic Taiwan.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37]

The most widely accepted theory of the population of the islands is the ["Out-of-Taiwan" model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out-of-Taiwan_hypothesis) that follows the Austronesian expansion during the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) in a series of maritime migrations originating from [Taiwan](25734.md) that spread to the islands of the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md); ultimately reaching as far as [New Zealand](4913064.md), [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island), and [Madagascar](18964.md).[^cite_note-Chambers2013-27][^cite_note-Bellwood2004-38] Austronesians themselves originated from the Neolithic rice-cultivating pre-Austronesian civilizations of the [Yangtze River delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Yangtze) in coastal southeastern China pre-dating the [conquest of those regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southward_expansion_of_the_Han_dynasty) by the [Han Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_Chinese).

This includes civilizations like the [Liangzhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liangzhu_culture), [Hemudu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemudu_culture), and [Majiabang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majiabang_culture) cultures.[^cite_note-Liu2012-39] It connects speakers of the [Austronesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) in a common linguistic and genetic lineage, including the [Taiwanese indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_indigenous_peoples), [Islander Southeast Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islander_Southeast_Asians), [Chams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chams), Islander [Melanesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesians), [Micronesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesian_people), [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians), and the [Malagasy people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malagasy_people). Aside from language and genetics, they also share common cultural markers like [multihull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multihull) and [outrigger boats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outrigger_boat), [tattooing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tattooing), [rice cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_cultivation), [wetland agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetland_agriculture), [teeth blackening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teeth_blackening), [jade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jade) carving, [betel nut chewing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paan), [ancestor worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestor_worship), and the same [domesticated plants and animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domesticated_plants_and_animals_of_Austronesia) (including dogs, pigs, chickens, yams, bananas, sugarcane, and coconuts).[^cite_note-Chambers2013-27][^cite_note-Bellwood2004-38][^cite_note-BlenchFruits-40]



The multiple waves of immigration the Philippines received, before the Protohistoric period.

&#x20;A 2021 genetic study examining representatives of 115 indigenous communities found evidence of at least five independent waves of early human migration. Negrito groups, divided between those in Luzon and those in Mindanao, may come from a single wave and diverged subsequently, or through two separate waves. This likely occurred sometime after 46,000 years ago. Another Negrito migration entered Mindanao sometime after 25,000 years ago. Two early East Asian waves were detected, one most strongly evidenced among the [Manobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manobo) people who live in inland Mindanao, and the other in the [Sama-Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sama-Bajau) and related people of the Sulu archipelago, Zamboanga Peninsula, and Palawan. The admixture found in the Sama people indicates a relationship with the [Htin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Htin_people) and [Mlabri people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mlabri_people) of mainland Southeast Asia, both peoples being speakers of an [Austroasiatic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austroasiatic_language) and reflects a similar genetic signal found in western Indonesia. These happened sometime after 15,000 years ago and 12,000 years ago respectively, around the time the [last glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_glacial_period) was coming to an end. Austronesians, either from Southern China or Taiwan, were found to have come in at least two distinct waves. The first, occurring perhaps between 10,000 and 7,000 years ago, brought the ancestors of indigenous groups that today live around the [Cordillera Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordillera_Central_\(Luzon\)) mountain range. Later migrations brought other Austronesian groups, along with agriculture, and the languages of these recent Austronesian migrants effectively replaced those existing populations. In all cases, new immigrants appear to have mixed to some degree with existing populations. The integration of Southeast Asia into Indian Ocean trading networks around 2,000 years ago also shows some impact, with South Asian genetic signals present within some Sama-Bajau communities. There is also some Papuan migration to Southeast Mindanao as Papuan genetic signatures were detected in the [Sangil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangil_language) and [Blaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaan_people) ethnic groups.[^cite_note-Larena-7]



A gold statue of the Hindu-Buddhist goddess ([Devata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devata)) "Tara" found in southern Philippines showing the incipient adoption of Hinduism and Buddhism, from [India](14533.md) and [China](5405.md).

&#x20;By 1000 BCE, the inhabitants of the Philippine archipelago had developed into four distinct kinds of peoples: tribal groups, such as the [Aetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeta_peoples), [Hanunoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanun%C3%B3'o_language), [Ilongots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilongot_people), and the [Mangyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangyan) who depended on [hunter-gathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) and were concentrated in forests; warrior societies, such as the [Isneg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isneg) and [Kalinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalinga_people) who practiced social ranking and [ritualized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritualization) warfare and roamed the plains; the petty [plutocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutocracy) of the [Ifugao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifugao_people) Cordillera Highlanders, who occupied the mountain ranges of [Luzon](69375.md); and the harbor principalities of the estuarine civilizations that grew along rivers and seashores while participating in trans-island maritime trade.[^cite_note-Legarda,_Benito,_Jr._2001_40-41] It was also during the first millennium BCE that early metallurgy was said to have reached the archipelagos of maritime Southeast Asia via trade with India[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunoz200645-42][^cite_note-43]

Around 300–700 CE, the seafaring peoples of the islands traveling in *[balangays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balangay)* began to trade with the [Indianized kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianized_kingdoms) in the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago) and the nearby [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian) principalities, adopting influences from both [Buddhism](3267529.md) and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism).[^cite_note-44]

### Maritime Jade Road



[Song Dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_Dynasty) Era (Year 900s) royal Porcelain-ware found in the KPC ("**Kalaga** (Caraga) **Putuan** (Butuan) **Crescent**") Region, Song China considered the Philippines a valuable source of **Jade**, Gold, Spices, and Incense, that they manufactured specially made Porcelain for the local Philippine kingdoms.

&#x20;The [Maritime Jade Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingling-o) was initially established by the animist indigenous peoples between the Philippines and Taiwan, and later expanded to cover Vietnam, Malaysia, Indonesia, Thailand, and other countries.[^cite_note-45] Artifacts made from white and green [nephrite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jade) have been discovered at a number of archeological excavations in the Philippines since the 1930s. The artifacts have been both tools like [adzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adze)[^cite_note-UCLA1981-46] and [chisels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chisel), and ornaments such as lingling-o earrings, bracelets, and beads.[^cite_note-uno-47] Tens of thousands were found in a single site in [Batangas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batangas).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott198417-48][^cite_note-Pathos_of_Origin-49] The jade is said to have originated nearby in Taiwan and is also found in many other areas in insular and mainland Southeast Asia. These artifacts are said to be evidence of long range communication between prehistoric Southeast Asian societies.[^cite_note-50] Throughout history, the Maritime Jade Road has been known as one of the most extensive sea-based trade networks of a single geological material in the prehistoric world, existing for 3,000 years from 2000 BCE to 1000 CE.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] The operations of the Maritime Jade Road coincided with an era of near absolute peace which lasted for 1,500 years, from 500 BCE to 1000 CE.[^cite_note-55] During this peaceful pre-colonial period, not a single burial site studied by scholars yielded any osteological proof for violent death. No instances of mass burials were recorded as well, signifying the peaceful situation of the islands. Burials with violent proof were only found from burials beginning in the 15th century, likely due to the newer cultures of expansionism imported from India and China. When the Spanish arrived in the 16th century, they recorded some warlike groups, whose cultures have already been influenced by the imported Indian and Chinese expansionist cultures of the 15th century.[^cite_note-56]

### The Sa Huỳnh culture

See also: [Kalanay Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalanay_Cave)



Asia in 200 BCE, showing the [Sa Huỳnh culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa_Hu%E1%BB%B3nh_culture) in [Mainland Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia) and the Philippines in transition.

The [Sa Huỳnh culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa_Hu%E1%BB%B3nh_culture) centered on present-day Vietnam, showed evidence of an extensive trade network. Sa Huỳnh beads were made from glass, [carnelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnelian), [agate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agate), [olivine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivine), [zircon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zircon), gold, and [garnet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garnet); most of these materials were not local to the region, and were most likely imported. [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty)-style [bronze mirrors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_mirror) were also found in Sa Huỳnh sites.

Conversely, Sa Huỳnh produced ear ornaments have been found in archaeological sites in [Central Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Thailand), Taiwan (Orchid Island), and in the Philippines, in the [Palawan](240395.md), [Tabon Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabon_Caves). One of the great examples is the [Kalanay Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalanay_Cave) in [Masbate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masbate); the artefacts on the site in one of the "Sa Huỳnh-Kalanay" pottery complex sites were dated 400BCE–1500 CE. The [Maitum anthropomorphic pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maitum_anthropomorphic_pottery) in the [Sarangani Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarangani_Province) of southern [Mindanao](20546.md) is c. 200 CE.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

Ambiguity of what is Sa Huỳnh culture puts into question its extent of influence in Southeast Asia. Sa Huỳnh culture is characterized by use of cylindrical or egg-shaped burial jars associated with hat-shaped lids. Using its mortuary practice as a new definition, Sa Huỳnh culture should be geographically restricted across Central Vietnam between Hue City in the north and Nha Trang City in the south. Recent archeological research reveals that the potteries in Kalanay Cave are quite different from those of the Sa Huỳnh but strikingly similar to those in Hoa Diem site, Central Vietnam, and Samui Island, Thailand. New estimate dates the artifacts in Kalanay cave to come much later than Sa Huỳnh culture at 200–300 CE. Bio-anthropological analysis of human fossils found also confirmed the colonization of Vietnam by Austronesian people from insular Southeast Asia in, e.g., the Hoa Diem site.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60]

## Precolonial period (AD 900 to 1565) – Independent polities

Locations of pre-colonial principalities, polities, kingdoms and sultanates in the Philippine archipelago

Also known to a lesser extent as the Pre-Philippines period, is a pre-unification period characterized by many independent states known as polities each with its own history, cultures, chieftains, and governments distinct from each other. According to sources from Southern Liang, people from the kingdom of [Langkasuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Langkasuka) in present-day [Thailand](30128.md) were wearing cotton clothes made in Luzon, Philippines as early as 516–520 CE.[^cite_note-61] The British Historian Robert Nicholl citing Arab chronicler Al Ya'akubi, had written that on the early years of the 800s, the kingdoms of Muja (then Pagan [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei)) and Mayd ([Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i)) waged war against the Chinese Empire.[^cite_note-Nicholl-62] Medieval Indian scholars also referred to the Philippines as "Panyupayana" (The lands surrounded by water).[^cite_note-Panyupayana-63]

By the 1300s, a number of the large coastal settlements had emerged as trading centers, and became the focal point of societal changes.[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] This phase of history can be noted for its highly mobile nature, with barangays transforming from being settlements and turning into fleets and vice versa, with the wood constantly re-purposed according to the situation.[^cite_note-Scott1994-64] Politics during this era was personality-driven and organization was based on shifting alliances and contested loyalties set in a backdrop of constant inter-polity interactions, both through war and peace.[^cite_note-\:7-15]

Legendary accounts often mention the interaction of early Philippine polities with Hindu Majapahit empire, but there is not much archaeological evidence to definitively support such a relationship.[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] Considerable evidence exists, on the other hand, for extensive trade with the [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya) empire.[^cite_note-JocanoJr2012-65]

The exact scope and mechanisms of Indian cultural influences on early Philippine polities are still the subject of some debate among Southeast Asian historiographers,[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10][^cite_note-Osborne2004-66] but the current scholarly consensus is that there was probably little or no direct trade between India and the Philippines,[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10][^cite_note-Osborne2004-66] and Indian cultural traits, such as linguistic terms and religious practices,[^cite_note-JocanoJr2012-65] filtered in during the 10th through the early 14th centuries, through early Philippine polities' relations with the Hindu Majapahit empire.[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] The Philippine archipelago is thus one of the countries, just at the outer edge of what is considered the "[Greater Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India) cultural zone".[^cite_note-Osborne2004-66]



A gold statuette of the Hindu-Buddhist goddess ([Devata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devata)) "Kinari" found in the [Caraga region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caraga_region) of the Philippines showing that conforming to ancient records, the area was ruled by Hindu Rajahs.

&#x20;The early polities of the Philippine archipelago were typically characterized by a three-tier social structure. Although different cultures had different terms to describe them, this three-tier structure invariably consisted of an apex nobility class, a class of "freemen", and a class of dependent debtor-bondsmen called."[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10][^cite_note-\:7-15] Among the members of the nobility class were leaders who held the political office of which was responsible for leading autonomous social groups called.[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] Whenever these banded together, either to form a larger settlement[^cite_note-Jocano2001-10] or a geographically looser alliance group,[^cite_note-\:7-15] the more senior or respected among them would be recognized as a "paramount datu", variedly called a Lakan, Sultan, Rajah, or simply a more senior Datu.[^cite_note-Scott1994-64][^cite_note-Jocano2001-10][^cite_note-Legarda,_Benito,_Jr._2001_40-41] Eventually, by the 14th to 16th century, inter-kingdom warfare escalated[^cite_note-67] and population densities across the archipelago was low.[^cite_note-68]

### Initial recorded history

During the period of the south Indian [Pallava dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty) and the north Indian [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire), Indian culture spread to Southeast Asia and the Philippines that led to the establishment of [Indianized kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indianized_kingdom).[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70]

The date inscribed in the oldest Philippine document found so far, the [Laguna Copperplate Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription), is 900 CE. From the details of the document, written in [Kawi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawi_script), the bearer of a debt, Namwaran, along with his children Lady Angkatan and Bukah, are cleared of a debt by the ruler of [Pila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pila_\(historical_polity\)) and [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)). It is the earliest document that shows the use of mathematics in precolonial Philippine societies. A standard system of weights and measures is demonstrated by the use of precise measurement for gold, and familiarity with rudimentary astronomy is shown by fixing the precise day within the month in relation to the phases of the moon.[^cite_note-manapat-71] From the various [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) terms and titles seen in the document, the culture and society of Manila Bay was that of a [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu)–[Old Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Malay) amalgamation, similar to the cultures of [Java](69336.md), [Peninsular Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Malaysia), and [Sumatra](28679.md) at the time.

There are no other significant documents from this period of precolonial Philippine society and culture until the [Doctrina Christiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctrina_Christiana) of the late 16th century, written at the start of the Spanish period in both native [Baybayin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baybayin) script and Spanish. Other artifacts with Kawi script and baybayin were found, such as an Ivory seal from [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butuan), dated to the early 10th–14th centuries,[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] and the [Calatagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calatagan) pot with baybayin inscription, dated to not later than early 16th century.[^cite_note-74]



A [Boxer Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxer_Codex) image illustrating the 1590s early Spanish colonial period Tagalog *[Maginoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maginoo)* (noble class).

In the years leading up to 1000, there were already several maritime societies existing in the islands but there was no unifying political [state](23604120.md) encompassing the entire Philippine archipelago. Instead, the region was dotted by numerous semi-autonomous *[barangays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precolonial_barangay)* (settlements ranging in size from villages to city-states) under the sovereignty of competing [thalassocracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalassocracy) ruled by [datus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datu), wangs, [rajahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah), [sultans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan) or [lakans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakan),[^cite_note-75] or by upland agricultural societies like that of the [Ifugao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifugao) and [Mangyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangyan).[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77] These polities included:

- [Pila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pila_\(historical_polity\))

- [Kingdom of Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_polity\))

- [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\))

- [Namayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namayan)

- [Cainta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cainta_\(historical_polity\))

- [Kumintang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumintang_\(historical_polity\))

- the Kingdom of Taytay in [Palawan](240395.md) (mentioned by [Antonio Pigafetta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Pigafetta) to be where they resupplied when the remaining ships escaped [Cebu](160278.md) after Magellan was slain)

- the Chieftaincy of [Coron Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coron_Island) ruled by fierce warriors called [Tagbanua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagbanwa_people) as reported by Spanish missionaries mentioned by Nilo S. Ocampo,

  [^cite_note-78]

- [Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan_\(historical_polity\))

- [Caboloan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caboloan)

- [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) and its vassal-states of [Sandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandao) and [Pulilu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulilu)

- [Madja-as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madja-as)

-

- [Bo-ol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo-ol)

- the Hindu kingdoms of [Sanmalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanmalan), [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan), and [Cebu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Cebu)

- the sultanates of [Buayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Buayan), [Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Maguindanao), [Lanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_Lanao), and [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Sultanate)

Some of these regions were part of the Malayan empires of [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit), and [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunoz2006171-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81]

### Tondo

Main articles: [Tondo (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)) and [History of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Manila)



The [Laguna Copperplate Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription), c. 900 CE. The oldest known historical record found in the Philippines, which indirectly refers to the polity of Tondo

Tondo was a major trade hub on Luzon island in the northern part of the Pasig River delta. The earliest historical record of local polities and kingdoms, the [Laguna Copperplate Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription), indirectly refers to the Tagalog polity of [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)) (c. before 900–1589) and two to three other settlements believed to be located somewhere near Tondo, as well as a settlement near Mt. Diwata in Mindanao, and the temple complex of Medang in Java.[^cite_note-Postma1992-82] Although the precise political relationships between these polities is unclear in the text of the inscription, the artifact is usually accepted as evidence of intra- and inter-regional political linkages as early as 900.[^cite_note-Postma1992-82][^cite_note-Scott1994-64][^cite_note-\:7-15] By the arrival of the earliest European ethnographers during the 1500s, Tondo was led by the paramount ruler called a "[Lakan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakandula)".[^cite_note-Scott1994-64][^cite_note-\:7-15] It had grown into a major trading hub, sharing a monopoly with the [Kingdom of Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_polity\)) over the trade of Ming dynasty[^cite_note-Ring-83] products throughout the archipelago.[^cite_note-Scott1994-64] This trade was significant enough that the [Yongle Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yongle_Emperor) appointed a Chinese governor named Ko Ch'a-lao to oversee it.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85]

Since at least the year 900, this thalassocracy centered in [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay) flourished via an active trade with Chinese, Japanese, Malays, and various other peoples in Asia. Tondo was led by a Lakan who belonged to the caste of the [Maginoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maginoo), who were the nobility in ancient Tagalog society.[^cite_note-86] The people of Tondo were also good agriculturists, and lived through farming and [aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture).

In 900 CE, the Lord of Pailah Jayadewa presented a document of debt forgiveness to Lady Angkatan and her brother Bukah, the children of Namwaran. This is described in the Philippines' oldest known document, the [Laguna Copperplate Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription).[^cite_note-Woods2006-87]

### Cainta

Main article: [Cainta (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cainta_\(historical_polity\))

Cainta was a fortified upriver polity in present-day [Rizal province](232458.md) that occupied both shores of an arm of the [Pasig River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasig_River). The river bisected it in the middle, a moat surrounded its log walls and stone [bulwarks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastion) armed with native cannons ([Lantakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lantaka)), and the city itself was encased by Bamboo thickets.[^cite_note-B\&R3Anonymous1572-88]

### Namayan

Main article: [Namayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namayan)



Map of Namayan (pink) under Lakantagkan according to the accounts of Felix Huerta. Calatondongan, Dibag, Pinacauasan and Yamagtogon are missing. Meycatmon's location is unclear.

Namayan, also a Pasig river polity, arose as a confederation of local [barangays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barangays).[^cite_note-Huerta-89] Local tradition says that it achieved its peak in the 11th–14th centuries.[^cite_note-Fox19772-90] Archeological findings in Santa Ana have produced the oldest evidence of continuous habitation among the Pasig-river polities, pre-dating artifacts found within the historical sites of [Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Maynila) and [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)).[^cite_note-Fox19772-90]

### Kumintang

Main article: [Kumintang (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumintang_\(historical_polity\))

Kumintang was a large polity around the [Calumpang River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calumpang_River) in modern-day [Batangas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batangas_City). According to local tradition, it was ruled by a legendary figure named Gat Pulintan, who refused to be Christianized and settled to the hills to take refuge and continue resistance against Spanish occupation. It became a Spanish town in 1581 and unofficially renamed as Batangan.[^cite_note-\:15-91][^cite_note-\:42-92]

### Pangasinan

Main article: [Pangasinan (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan_\(historical_polity\))



Probable Citizens of the Sinified [Wangdom of Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangasinan_\(historical_polity\)) 漢文 "Feng-chia-hsi-lan", with [kampilan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampilan) sword, shown in the [Boxer Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxer_Codex) (1590) surmised to come from Taimei Anchorage, [Lingayen Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingayen_Gulf), [Luzon](69375.md)

Places in Pangasinan like [Lingayen Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingayen_Gulf) were mentioned as early as 1225, when Lingayen as known was Li-ying-tung had been listed in Chao Ju-kua's *Chu Fan Chih* (An account of the various barbarians) as one of the trading places along with Mai (Mindoro or Manila).[^cite_note-93] In northern Luzon, Pangasinan) (c. 1406–1576) sent emissaries to China in 1406–1411 as a tributary-state,[^cite_note-autogenerated8-94] and it also traded with Japan.[^cite_note-scott1989bp8-95] Chinese records of this kingdom, named Feng-chia-hsi-lan (Pangasinan), and was notable for its salt-making industry, began when the first tributary King (Wang in Chinese), Kamayin, sent an envoy offering gifts to the Chinese Emperor.[^cite_note-scott1989bp8-95] The state occupies the current province of [Pangasinan](1691800.md). It flourished around the same period, the [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya) and [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit) empires arose in Indonesia which had extended their influence to much of the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago). Pangasinan enjoyed full independence until the Spanish conquest.

In the sixteenth century Pangasinan was called the "Port of Japan" by the Spanish. The locals wore native apparel typical of other maritime Southeast Asian ethnic groups in addition to Japanese and Chinese silks. Even common people were clad in Chinese and Japanese cotton garments. They also blackened their teeth and were disgusted by the white teeth of foreigners, which were likened to that of animals. Also, used porcelain jars typical of Japanese and Chinese households. Japanese-style gunpowder weapons were also encountered in naval battles in the area.[^cite_note-Scott1994-64] In exchange for these goods, traders from all over Asia would come to trade primarily for gold and slaves, but also for deerskins, civet, and other local products. Other than a notably more extensive trade network with Japan and China, they were culturally similar to other Luzon groups to the south.

### Ma-i

Main article: [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i)



A collection of gold [Piloncitos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piloncitos) stamped with the Baybayin character for "Ma" possibly representing the nation of Ma-i.

Arab chronicler Al Ya'akubi, had written that in the 800s, the kingdoms of Muja (Then Pagan/Hindu Brunei) and Mayd (Ma-i) militarily competed with the Chinese Empire.[^cite_note-Nicholl-62] Volume 186 of the [official history of the Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Song_\(Yuan_dynasty\)) describes the polity of [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) (c. before 971 – after 1339). Song dynasty traders visited Ma-i annually, and their accounts described Ma-i's geography, trade products, and the trade behaviors of its rulers.[^cite_note-KansaiICIS-96] Chinese merchants noted that Ma-i's citizens were honest and trustworthy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott198467-97] Because the descriptions of Mai's location in these accounts are unclear, there is dispute about Mai's location, with some scholars believing it was located in [Bay, Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay,_Laguna),[^cite_note-\:11-12] and others believing it was on the island of [Mindoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindoro).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott1984\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2018\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2018)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-98] The Buddhist polity traded with [Ryukyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryukyu) and Japan.[^cite_note-99] [Chao Jukua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chao_Jukua), a customs inspector in [Fukien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujian_Province,_Republic_of_China) province, China wrote the *[Zhufan Zhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhu_fan_zhi)* ("Description of the Barbarous Peoples").[^cite_note-100] William Henry Scott said, that unlike other Philippine kingdoms or polities which needed backing from the Chinese Imperial Court to attract commerce, the Polity of Ma-i was powerful enough to have no need to send tributes to the Chinese throne.[^cite_note-101] According to commercial receipts, Ma-i's industries specialized in the export of "kapok cotton, yellow bees-wax, tortoise shell, medicinal betel nuts, and cloth of various patterns."[^cite_note-Nicholl-62]

### Sandao

Main article: [Sandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandao)



An example of a Jade [Lingling-o](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingling-o) from [Palawan](240395.md) island, Sandao "三嶋", which included Palawan, was a source of high quality carved Jade that was exported towards the markets found westwards of the Philippines.

&#x20;Sandao "三嶋" in Chinese characters, which was also known as Sanyu (三嶼), was a Prehispanic Filipino nation recorded in Chinese annals as a nation occupying the islands of Jiamayan 加麻延 (present-day [Calamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamianes)), Balaoyou 巴姥酉 (present-day [Palawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palawan_Island)),[^cite_note-102] and Pulihuan 蒲裏喚 (near present-day [Manila](184334.md)).[^cite_note-103] In the Chinese Gazetteer the Zhufan zhi 諸蕃志 (1225), it was described as a vassal-state of the more powerful nation of [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) centered in nearby [Mindoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindoro).[^cite_note-storymaps.arcgis.com-104]

### Pulilu

Main article: [Pulilu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulilu)



An illustration from the Boxer Codex depicts the war tattoos of Cebuano men, which served as prestigious symbols of bravery. These "Pintados" (painted ones) who were primarily [Visayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayans), of which the people of the [Rajahnate of Cebu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Cebu) were the leaders of the alliance, only received tattoos following successes in combat. This tradition links them to the [Baiyue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baiyue) of China—a maritime culture celebrated for its warrior spirit and recurring use of dragon and serpent motifs in their body art.

[^cite_note-105]

Pulilu was a Prehispanic polity centered at [Polillo, Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polillo,_Quezon)[^cite_note-106] and was mentioned in the Chinese Gazeteer Zhufan zhi 諸蕃志 (1225). It is described as politically connected to the nation of [Sandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandao) "三嶋" at the [Calamianes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamianes) which itself was a vassal-state to the larger country of [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) "麻逸" centered in Mindoro. Its people were recorded to be warlike, and prone to pillaging and conflict. In this area, the sea is full of coral reefs, which have wavy surfaces that resemble decaying tree trunks or razor blades. Ships going by the reefs must be ready to make sharp maneuvers to avoid them because they are sharper than swords and halberds. Red coral and blue langgan coral are also produced here; however, they are quite difficult to find. It is also similar to the nation of [Sandao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandao) in local customs and trade products. The chief export of this small polity are rare corals.

### Visayan belligerence against Imperial China

Antonio Pigafetta, the expedition scribe, enumerated the towns and dependencies Cebu had.[^cite_note-auto2-107]

Writing in the 13th century, the Chinese historian [Chao Ju-Kua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhao_Rukuo) mentioned raids conducted by the *Pi-sho-ye* on the port cities of southern China between A.D. 1174–1190, which he believed came by way of the southern portion of the island of [Taiwan](25734.md).[^cite_note-108] Subsequent historians identified these raiders as Visayans from the [Visayas](72418.md) islands while the historian Efren B. Isorena, through analysis of historical accounts and wind currents in the Pacific side of East and Southeast Asia, concluded that said raiders were most likely the people of Ibabao (the precolonial name for the eastern coast and a portion of the northern coast of Samar).[^cite_note-109]

### Madja-as

Main article: [Madja-as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madja-as)



Images from the [Boxer Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxer_Codex) illustrating an 1590's early Spanish colonial period *kadatuan* or [tumao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tumao) (noble class) [Visayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayan) couple.



A [royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_family) couple of the Visayans.

One theory espoused by some historians is that ten exiled [datus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Datu) of the collapsing empire of [Srivijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srivijaya)[^cite_note-110] led by Datu Puti migrated to the central islands of the Philippines, fleeing from Rajah Makatunaw of the island of [Borneo](4518.md). Upon reaching the island of [Panay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panay) and purchasing the island from Negrito chieftain Marikudo, they established a confederation of polities and named it [Madja-as](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madja-as) and they settled the surrounding islands of the [Visayas](72418.md). This is according to Pedro Monetclaro's book [Maragtas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maragtas).[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] However, the actual personage of Rajah Makatunaw was mentioned in earlier Chinese texts about Brunei dating him to 1082, when he was the descendant of Seri Maharaja and he was accompanied by Sang Aji (the ancestor of Sultan Muhammad Shah). There is thus a disparity of dates between the Maragtas Book (based on oral legends) and the Chinese texts.[^cite_note-PreislamicBruneiKings-114] Historian Robert Nicholl also positively identify the pre-Islamic Bruneian Buddhist kingdom of Vijayapura, itself a Bornean tributary of the Srivijaya Empire in Palembang, and in earlier times was a rump state in Sarawak of the fallen [Funan Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Funan) formerly at what is now Cambodia,[^cite_note-115]<sup>: 36 </sup> this was the ancestral homeland of the Visayans of the 10 Datus of Panay.[^cite_note-116] Furthermore, he identified the Rajah Makatunao mentioned in the Maragtas book with Rajah Tugau of the Melano nation centered in [Sarawak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sarawak). Augustinian Friar Rev. Fr. Santaren recorded that Datu Macatunao or Rajah Makatunao who was the "sultan of the Moros," and a relative of Datu Puti who seized the properties and riches of the ten datus was eventually killed by the warriors named Labaodungon and Paybare, using native Filipino and Bornean recruits. This, after learning of this injustice from their father-in-law Paiburong, sailed to Odtojan in Borneo where Makatunaw ruled. The warriors sacked the city, killed Makatunaw and his family, retrieved the stolen properties of the 10 datus, enslaved the remaining population of Odtojan, and sailed back to Panay. Labaw Donggon and his wife, Ojaytanayon, later settled in a place called Moroboro. Afterwards, the datus in Panay, other Visayan islands, and southern Luzon were said to have founded various towns.[^cite_note-Talaguit-117]

### Cebu (historical polity)

Main article: [Cebu (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebu_\(historical_polity\))



A picture of a Bronze Image of the Hindu God [Shiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva) (lost during World War 2), found at Mactan-Cebu. It shows how the culture of the area was Hindu and [Indianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India).

The Kingdom of Cebu was a precolonial state. It was founded by Sri Lumay otherwise known as Rajamuda Lumaya, who was a half Malay half Tamil (South Indian) from the island of [Sumatra](28679.md), a colony of [Rajendra I's Indian-Tamil conquest of Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South-East_Asia_campaign_of_Rajendra_I).[^cite_note-Santarita-118] The Chinese recorded the name of the Rajahanate of Cebu as 'Sokbu' (束務) in [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien) or 'Suwu' in Mandarin.[^cite_note-CarmelaAng-119] A kingdom of the same name as Suwu was mentioned to have existed as early as the year 1225, according to the Chinese Annals the Zhufan Zhi (諸蕃志)[^cite_note-Shao-yun-120] and the later 17th Century Chinese traders to the Philippines referred to Cebu using the same term, it is thus presumed to be the same location.[^cite_note-CarmelaAng-119] The Indianized royalty of Cebu ruled the native Cebuano people from the [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_script)-[Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) labeled capital, [Singhapala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhapala) (சிங்கப்பூர்)[^cite_note-Astrid-121] which is Tamil-Sanskrit[^cite_note-122] for "Lion City", the same root words as with the modern city-state of [Singapore](27318.md). This rajahnate warred against the 'magalos' (slave traders) of [Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maguindanao) and had an alliance with the [Rajahnate of Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan) and Indianized [Kutai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutai) in South Borneo, before it was weakened by the insurrection of Datu [Lapulapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapulapu).[^cite_note-Panyupayana-63] The kingdom enjoyed the diplomatic recognition of Thailand as observed by Ferdinand Magellan's expedition which noted an embassy borne by a ship from Siam ([Thailand](30128.md)) that had landed at the Rajahnate and had tributes meant for Rajah Humabon.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] Goods exported from Cebu include: rice, millet, panicum, maize, figs, oranges, lemons, sugar-canes, cocos, gourds, ginger, honey, and other such things; also palm-wine and some gold.[^cite_note-auto2-107]

### Butuan

Main article: [Butuan (historical polity)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butuan_\(historical_polity\))



The [Butuan Ivory Seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butuan_Ivory_Seal), displayed at the [National Museum of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_the_Philippines). The [Kawi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawi_script) lettering says "But-wan" and the smaller lettering (similar to [Baybayin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baybayin)) says "Bu-wa" (diacritics for the "Wan/Ban" in Kawi and "Bu/Ba" in the smaller letters have worn off).

The official history of the Song dynasty next refers to the [Rajahnate of Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan) (c. before 1001–1756) in northeastern Mindanao which is the first polity from the Philippine archipelago recorded as having sent a tribute mission to the Chinese empire—on March 17, 1001, CE. Butuan later attained prominence under the rule of Rajah Sri Bata Shaja.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECruzAdiong2020-125] In the year 1011, [Rajah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah) Sri Bata Shaja, the monarch of the [Indianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India) [Rajahnate of Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan), a maritime-state famous for its goldwork and [metallurgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallurgy) industry plus its manufacture of musical instruments[^cite_note-126] sent a trade envoy under ambassador Likan-shieh to the Chinese Imperial Court demanding equal diplomatic status with other states.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott198459-127] The request being approved, it opened up direct commercial links with the Rajahnate of Butuan and the Chinese Empire thereby diminishing the monopoly on Chinese trade previously enjoyed by their rivals, [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tondo) and the [Champa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa) civilization.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECruzAdiong202024-128] Evidence of the existence of this rajahnate is given by the [Butuan Silver Paleograph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butuan_Silver_Paleograph).[^cite_note-129] Researcher Eric Casino, believes the name of the first Rajah mentioned in Chinese records, Rajah Kiling, is not Visayan in origin but rather, Indian, because Kiling refers to the people of India.[^cite_note-130] The [Sejarah Melayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sejarah_Melayu) (Malay Annals) of the nearby country of Malaysia, refers to the similarly worded [Keling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keling) as immigrant people from [India](14533.md).[^cite_note-sabrizain-131]

### Sanmalan

Main article: [Sanmalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanmalan)



The *[Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama)*, chronicled the rise of the Java-centered [Majapahit Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit_Empire) and its conquest of the kingdom of Solot ([Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)), which then rebelled and sacked the Majapahit province of Pon-i ([Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Brunei#Hindu_Majapahit_invasion_of_Borneo)).

At the same time as the rise of Butuan was the emergence of Sanmalan. Sanmalan was a precolonial Philippine kingdom on what is now [Zamboanga](25167165.md).[^cite_note-132] Known in Chinese records as "Sanmalan" 三麻蘭. The Chinese chronicled during 982, a tribute from its Rajah or King, Chulan, represented at the imperial court by ambassador Ali Bakti. It transshipped goods from [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) for local consumption, trading in products like: aromatics, dates, glassware, ivory, peaches, refined sugar, and rose- water.[^cite_note-133] In the 1200s, the Chinese chronicle Zhufan zhi (諸蕃志) recorded its change from a trade emporium to slaving state as Zamboanga began waging war and raiding its neighboring kingdoms in [Borneo](4518.md), [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(900%E2%80%931565\)), [Sulawesi](28740.md), and [Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternate); for slaves to sell in [Java](69336.md).[^cite_note-storymaps.arcgis.com-104]

### Influence of the Madjapahit onto the Philippines

During the 1300s, the Chinese annals, *Nanhai zhi*, reported that Brunei invaded or administered [Sarawak](28258.md) and [Sabah](28678.md) as well as the Philippine kingdoms of [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajahnate_of_Butuan), [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu), and [Ma-i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma-i) (Mindoro) which would regain their independence at a later date.[^cite_note-134] Afterwards, the Javanese-centered Hindu empire of [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit), in turn invaded Brunei and had briefly ruled the [Sulu Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Archipelago) as recorded in the epic poem [Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama), which stated that they controlled Solot ([Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERausa-Gomez1967Lourdes_Rausa-Gomez_cited_Sir_Stamford_Raffles,_himself_citing_the_'Traditional_History_of_Java'_wherein_he_said_that_Manila_and_Sulu_in_the_Philippines_were_part_of_Majapahapit,_however_she_doubted_the_veracity_of_Stamford_Raffles_assertion_due_to_the_lack_of_archaeological_evidence_between_Majapahit_and_the_Philippines_in_her_1967_article._However,_that_article_has_been_renderred_outdated_due_to_the_discovery_of_the_Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription_in_1989_which_proved_links_between_Java_and_Manila,_which_makes_her_dismissal_of_the_Raffles_assertion_null_and_the_Raffles_assertion_feasible-135]

Eventually, Sulu reestablished independence, and in vengeance, [assaulted the Majapahit province of Poni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_Philippines) ([Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei)) before a fleet from the capital drove them out.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERausa-Gomez196792-137]

> According to Javanese records a Javanese force expelled Sulu marauders from Brunei during the reign of Angka Wijaya he the last king to reign over Majapahit. The inhabitants of the Soeloe Islands (in the present Philippines) made an attack against Brunei (in order to obtain camphor), in keeping with their (piratical) nature, but they were driven off by the Javanese soldiers.

— Stamford Raffles

Sulu reaction against Majapahit Imperialism did not stop with the sacking of Poni (Brunei) as Sulu also invaded North and [East Kalimantan](453651.md) in Borneo, which were former Majapahit territories.[^cite_note-138] The subsequent start of the Islamic era ushered the slow death of [Majapahit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majapahit) as its provinces eventually seceded and became independent sultanates. With the upsurge of Islam, the remnants of Hindu Majapahit eventually fled to the island of [Bali](4147.md).[^cite_note-139]

&#x20;In Luzon, citing Kapampangan oral legends, [Nick Joaquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Joaquin) wrote about a princess of [Namayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namayan) named [Sasaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empress_Sasaban) who married the Emperor of Majapahit, locally known as Soledan and is allegedly the Maharajah Anka Widyaya.[^cite_note-Odal2000-140]Sultanate of Sulu



The banner of the Sultanate of Sulu

Main article: [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)

In 1380, [Karim ul' Makdum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makhdum_Karim) and Shari'ful Hashem Syed Abu Bakr, an [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) trader born in [Johore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor,_Malaysia), [Malaysia](3607937.md); arrived in [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Island) from [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malacca) and established the [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu) by converting its previous ruler, the Hindu king, [Rajah Baguinda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Baguinda), to Islam and then marrying his daughter. This sultanate eventually gained great wealth due to its diving for fine [pearls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl).[^cite_note-141] Before Islamization, the then Rajahnate of Sulu was established by Visayan speaking Hindu migrants from the Rajahnate of Butuan to the Sulu Archipelago as Tausug, the language of the Sulu state is classified as a Southern Visayan language.[^cite_note-142] Then Hindu Sulu started off as a spice-[entrepôt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrep%C3%B4t) for mutual trade with their cousins in the Butuan Rajahnate as well as the [Champa civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa) across the sea, in the 10th to 13th Centuries. Champa which is located in [Central Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Vietnam#Golden_Age_of_Cham_Civilization_and_wars_with_Angkor_Empire_\(7th_century%E2%80%931203\)) and the port-kingdom of [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu) traded with each other which resulted in Cham merchants settling in Sulu where they were known as Orang Dampuan. The Orang Dampuan were slaughtered by envious native Sulu Buranuns due to the wealth of the Orang Dampuan.[^cite_note-143] The Buranun were then subjected to retaliatory slaughter by the Orang Dampuan. Harmonious commerce between Sulu and the Orang Dampuan was later restored and the Orang Dampuans became the ancestors of the local [Yakan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakan_people).[^cite_note-144] The Yakans were descendants of the Taguima-based Orang Dampuan who came to Sulu from Champa.[^cite_note-145] As told before, Sulu was also briefly ruled under the Hindu Majapahit empire as narrated in the [Nagarakretagama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nagarakretagama) but afterwards, Sulu rebelled and sacked Brunei which was a nearby loyal province of Majapahit as Sulu extended its conquest to the former Majapahit territory of East and North Kalimantan. However, with the onset of Islam by the 15th century, they associated themselves with their new Arab-descended sultans whose origins was in Malacca and their fellow co-religionist Moros (ethnic groups of the Philippine who had accepted Islam) than their still Hindu, Visayan-speaking cousins. This culminated with royal intermarriages between the families of the then newly Islamized Maynila, as well the Sultanates of Brunei, Sulu, and Malacca.[^cite_note-cia-146]

### Sultanate of Maguindanao

Main article: [Sultanate of Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Maguindanao)

The [Sultanate of Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Maguindanao) rose to prominence at the end of the 15th century or the beginning of the 16th century, [Shariff Mohammed Kabungsuwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_Kabungsuwan) of [Johor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor), [Malaysia](3607937.md): introduced Islam in the island of Mindanao and he subsequently married Paramisuli, an [Iranun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranun_people) princess from Mindanao, and established the Sultanate of Maguindanao.[^cite_note-147]

It ruled most parts of coastal Mindanao and continued to exist prior to the Spanish colonialization until the 19th century. The Sultanate also traded and maintained good relations with the Chinese, Dutch, and the British.[^cite_note-National_Historical_Commission-148][^cite_note-Hayase2007-149] The Sultanate's main exports include: [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice), [wax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wax), [tobacco](30942.md), and [clove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clove) and [cinnamon barks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), alongside [coconut oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut_oil), [sago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sago), beans, [tortoiseshells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tortoiseshell), [bird's nests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edible_bird's_nest), and [ebony hardwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xanthostemon_verdugonianus).[^cite_note-Laarhoven_1986_35-150]

### Confederate States of Lanao



A performance of the Maranao royal dance, the "[Singkil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singkil)".

Main article: [Confederate States of Lanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_Lanao)

The Sultanates of Lanao in Mindanao, Philippines were founded in the 16th century through the influence of Shariff Kabungsuan, who was enthroned as first Sultan of Maguindanao in 1515. Islam was introduced to the area by Muslim missionaries and traders from the Middle East, Indian, and Malay regions who propagated Islam to Sulu and Maguindanao. Unlike in Sulu and Maguindanao, the Sultanate system in Lanao was uniquely decentralized. The area was divided into Four Principalities of Lanao or the Pat a Pangampong a Ranao which are composed of a number of royal houses (Sapolo ago Nem a Panoroganan or The Sixteen (16) Royal Houses) with specific territorial jurisdictions within mainland Mindanao. This decentralized structure of royal power in Lanao was adopted by the founders, and maintained up to the present day, in recognition of the shared power and prestige of the ruling clans in the area, emphasizing the values of unity of the nation (kaiisaisa o bangsa), patronage (kaseselai) and fraternity (kapapagaria). By the 16th century, Islam had spread to other parts of the Visayas and Luzon.

### The Bruneian Empire and the expansion of Islam



Territorial extent of the Bruneian Empire

Main article: [Bruneian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire)

Upon the secession of Poni (Brunei) from the Majapahit Empire, they imported the Arab Emir from Mecca, [Sharif Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_Ali), and became an independent Sultanate. During the reign of his descendant, Sultan [Bolkiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolkiah), in 1485 to 1521, he married Laila Menchanai, the daughter of Sulu Sultan Amir Ul-Ombra to expand Brunei's influence in both Luzon and Mindanao. Eventually, [Rajah Salalila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Salalila) of Maynila married the daughter of Sultan Bolkiah and Puteri Laila Menchanai of Sulu, placing Maynila under the influence of Brunei.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott1984-151] The new dynasty under the Islamized Rajah Salalila was established to challenge the House of Lakandula in Tondo.[^cite_note-Santiago-152][^cite_note-153]

Furthermore, Islam was further strengthened by the arrival to the Philippines of traders and [proselytizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselytizer) from Malaysia and Indonesia. The invasion of Brunei spread Chinese royalty such as [Ong Sum Ping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ong_Sum_Ping)'s kin and companions plus Arab dynasties such as the clan of Sultan [Sharif Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_Ali) and allies to the Philippines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAgoncillo199022-154] Brunei was so powerful that it already subjugated their Hindu Bornean neighbor, Kutai, to the south, though Kutai survived through a desperate alliance with Hindu Butuan and Cebu which were already struggling against encroaching Islamic powers like Maguindanao. Brunei had also gained influence over the northern third and the southern third of the Philippines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaunders200260-155][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerbertMilner198999-156][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELeaMilward200116-157][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHicks200734-158][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChurch201216-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEur2002203-160][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdul_Majid20072-161][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelman20138-162] Sultan Bolkiah is associated with the legend of Nakhoda Ragam the singing captain, a myth about a handsome, virile, strong, musically gifted and angelic voiced prince who is known for his martial exploits. There is contextual evidence that Sultan Bolkiah may indeed be Nakhoda Ragam, since he is of half Visayan-Filipino descent since later Spanish accounts record that Filipinos, especially Visayans, were obsessed with singing and the [warrior castes were particularly known for their great singing abilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madja-as).[^cite_note-163]

### The Lucoes

Main article: [Lucoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucoes)



Ruins of the Royal Palace of Ayutthaya, in the [Ayutthaya Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Historical_Park). Ayutthaya (Thailand) was the setting of the [Burmese-Siamese Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1547%E2%80%931549\)) where Lucoes from [Luzon](69375.md), Philippines were used as soldiers by both sides.

Concurrent with the spread of Islam in the Philippine archipelago was the rise of the [Lucoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucoes), or *Luzones*, who were the people of [Luzon](69375.md). They rose to prominence by establishing overseas communities all across [Southeast Asia](28741.md) as well as maintaining relations with [South](21566765.md) and [East](19605700.md) Asia, participating in trading ventures, navigation expeditions, and military campaigns in [Burma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taungoo_dynasty),[^cite_note-MediterraneanConnection-164] In 1505, Portuguese naval captains noted the presence of *Luzam* (Luzones from [Luzon island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon_island)) mercenaries and sailors, working aboard Portuguese ships off the [Malabar Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabar_Coast) in [Western India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_India), performing as hired muscle men, for the Portuguese war effort against the local Islamic kingdoms of India.[^cite_note-Luzam-165] Afterwards, Lucoes warriors aided the Burmese king in his invasion of Siam in 1547. At the same time, Lusung warriors fought alongside the Siamese king and faced the same Burmese Army.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-166] They were also in [Japan](15573.md), [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei), [Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Malacca), [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor), and [Sri Lanka](26750.md)[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-\:4-19] where they were employed as traders and mercenaries.[^cite_note-\:8-168][^cite_note-Scott1994-64][^cite_note-Reid-169] One prominent Luções was [Regimo de Raja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regimo_de_Raja), who was a spice magnate and a [Temenggung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temenggung) ([Jawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawi_script): تمڠݢوڠ)[^cite_note-turnbull-170] (Governor and Chief General) in Portuguese Malacca. He was also the head of an international armada which traded and protected commerce between the [Indian Ocean](14580.md), the [Strait of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca), the [South China Sea](74209.md),[^cite_note-171] and the [medieval maritime principalities of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(900%E2%80%931521\)).[^cite_note-\:7-15]

Pinto noted that there were a number of Luzones in the Islamic fleets that went to battle with the Portuguese in the Philippines during the 16th century. The Sultan of Aceh together with the Ottoman commander Heredim Mafamede whose uncle was the Viceroy of Egypt, assigned [Luzones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzones) to defend Aceh, and gave one of them, Sapetu Diraja, the task of holding Aru (northeast Sumatra) in 1540. Pinto also says one was named leader of the Malays remaining in the Moluccas Islands after the Portuguese conquest in 1511.[^cite_note-Pinto-172] Pigafetta notes that one of them was in command of the Brunei fleet in 1521.[^cite_note-\:4-19]



A [Luzon jar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon_jar) displayed in the Madrid Museum, Luzon Jars were highly prized in Japan as fermentation and taste enhancers of Japanese [Matcha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matcha) (Green Tea), due to the clay used formed out of the Philippines' unique volcanic soil. The Luzon Jars were used in the formal and sacred Japanese [Zen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zen_Buddhist) [Tea Ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Ceremony)

[^cite_note-173]

&#x20;which was also prized due to the [Wabi Sabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wabi_Sabi) aesthetics the Luzon Jars had embodied.

[^cite_note-inu-174]

However, the Luzones did not only fight on the side of the Muslims. Pinto says they were also apparently among the natives of the Philippines who fought the Muslims in 1538.[^cite_note-Pinto-172]

The Luzones were also pioneer seafarers, and it is recorded that the Portuguese were not only witnesses but also direct beneficiaries of Lusung's involvement. Many Luzones chose Malacca as their base of operations because of its strategic importance. When the Portuguese finally took Malacca in 1512, the resident Luzones held important government posts in the former sultanate. They were also large-scale exporters and ship owners that regularly sent junks to China, Brunei, Sumatra, Siam, and Sunda. One Lusung official by the name of Surya Diraja annually sent 175 tons of pepper to China and had to pay the Portuguese 9000 cruzados in gold to retain his plantation. His ships became part of the first Portuguese fleet that paid an official visit to the Chinese empire in 1517.[^cite_note-175] Furthermore, the [Boxer Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxer_Codex) said that: "The Luções, called Lequios, bring gold and cotton from their land, and trade Chinese silk and porcelain"[^cite_note-176] It is also recorded that every year, the Luções load Canton with 175 casks of pepper.[^cite_note-177] In addition to this, they also brought tortoise-shell and resins from their coast, which fetched a high price in China.[^cite_note-178] Overall, "In the fairs of Malacca, the Luções were famed merchants of pepper and gold, even exchanging them for Chinese silk." As Fernão Lopes de Castanheda writes of them.[^cite_note-179]

On [Mainland Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia), Luzones aided the Burmese king in his invasion of Siam in 1547. At the same time, Luzones fought alongside the Siamese king and faced the same elephant army of the Burmese king in the defence of the Siamese capital at Ayuthaya.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-166] Diogo do Couto wrote of them as such: "Under their chief Balagtas, 300 Luções fought for the King of Siam against Burmese invaders—so effective that the Siamese granted them land." The fact that the Thai/Siamese king granted them land and ennobled them attest to their admirable performance.[^cite_note-180] Lucoes military and trade activity reached as far as [Sri Lanka](26750.md) in the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) where Lungshanoid pottery made in Luzon were discovered in burials.[^cite_note-181]



An example of a Kris Sword, [Luzones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzones) soldiers from Luzon-Philippines were so adept in Kris wielding and martial arts, they became the Sultanate of Aceh's royal guards.

&#x20;The Portuguese were soon relying on Luzones bureaucrats for the administration of Malacca and on Luzones warriors, ships, and pilots for their military and commercial ventures in East Asia.

It was through the Luzones who regularly sent ships to China that the Portuguese discovered the ports of Canton in 1514. And it was on Luzones ships that the Portuguese were able to send their first diplomatic mission to China 1517. The Portuguese had the Luzones to thank for when they finally established their base at Macao in the mid-1500s.[^cite_note-\:8-168]

The Luzones were also instrumental in guiding Portuguese ships to discover Japan. The Western world first heard of Japan through the Portuguese. But it was through the Luzones that the Portuguese had their first encounter with the Japanese. The Portuguese king commissioned his subjects to get good pilots that could guide them beyond the seas of China and Malacca. In 1540, the Portuguese king's factor in Brunei, Brás Baião, recommended to his king the employment of Lusung pilots because of their reputation as "discoverers."[^cite_note-182] Thus it was through Luzones navigators that Portuguese ships found their way to Japan in 1543. The Luzones so impressed the Portuguese soldier, Joao de Barros, he considered the Luzones who were militarily and commercially active across the region, "the most warlike and valiant of these parts."[^cite_note-183] Meanwhile, in the nearby [Sultanate of Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Aceh) the Luções fighting men so impressed the Sultan, that they were assigned to become the Sultan's royal guard[^cite_note-184] and to be assigned as the Sultan's royal guard, is proof of Luçoes men's physical strength, martial prowess, and [masculine attractiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masculine_beauty_ideal); as during that time period, among medieval kingdoms, that office was delegated only to the most strong, intelligent, handsome, attractive, virile, aristocratic, and combat-worthy, of warriors.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186]

Filipinos from the island of Luzon (Lucoes) were not the only Filipinos abroad, historian William Henry Scott, quoting the Portuguese manuscript Summa Orientalis, noted that [Mottama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mottama) in [Burma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burma) (Myanmar) had a large presence of merchants from the island of Mindanao.[^cite_note-187]

### Rise and fall of Bo-ol

Main article: [Bo-ol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo-ol)

&#x20;Around 1563, at the closing stages of the precolonial era, the [Bo-ol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dapitan_Kingdom) achieved prominence and it was known to a later Spanish missionary, Alcina, as the "Venice of the Visayas", because it was a wealthy, wooden, and floating city-state in the Visayas. However, this kingdom was eventually attacked and destroyed by soldiers from the [Sultanate of Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ternate), a state made up of Muslim [Moluccans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moluccans). The survivors of the destruction, led by their datu, Pagbuaya, migrated to northern Mindanao and established a new settlement in the region known as Dapitan.&#x20;



A collection of Philippine *[lantaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lantaka)*, a type of swivel-gun used in inter-kingdom wars.

They then waged war against the Sultanate of Lanao and settled in the lands conquered from them. Eventually, in vengeance against the Muslims and Portuguese allied to the Ternateans, they aided the Spanish in the conquest of Muslim Manila and in the Spanish expeditions to capture Portuguese Ternate.

### Inter-kingdom rivalries
