During this period there was also a simmering territorial conflict between the polity of Tondo and Maynila, to which the ruler of Maynila, [Rajah Matanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Matanda), sought military assistance against Tondo from his relatives at the Sultanate of Brunei.[^cite_note-188] The Hindu Rajahnates of Butuan and Cebu also endured slave raids from, and waged wars against the Sultanate of Maguindanao.[^cite_note-marivir-189] Simultaneous with these slave-raids, was the rebellion of Datu [Lapulapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapulapu) of [Mactan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mactan) against [Rajah Humabon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Humabon) of Cebu.[^cite_note-190] The population was sparse due to warfare and also due to the common [frequency of typhoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon#Frequency) and the Philippines' location on the [Pacific ring of fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_ring_of_fire).[^cite_note-jstor.org-191] The multiple states competing over the limited territory and people of the islands simplified Spanish [colonialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonialization) by allowing its [conquistadors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquistador) to effectively employ a strategy of [divide and conquer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divide_and_rule) for rapid conquest.

## Spanish settlement and rule (1565–1898)

Main articles: [History of the Philippines (1565–1898)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1565%E2%80%931898\)), [Captaincy General of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_the_Philippines), and [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies)

### Early Spanish expeditions and conquests

Main articles: [Ruy López de Villalobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruy_L%C3%B3pez_de_Villalobos) and [Spanish–Moro conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93Moro_conflict)

A Spanish expedition around the world led by Portuguese explorer [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan) sighted [Samar Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samar_Island) but anchored off [Suluan Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suluan) on March 16, 1521. They landed the next day on [Homonhon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homonhon) Island, now part of [Guiuan, Eastern Samar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guiuan,_Eastern_Samar). Magellan claimed the islands he saw for Spain and named them Islas de San Lázaro. He established friendly relations with some of the local leaders especially with [Rajah Humabon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Humabon) and converted some of them to [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines). In the Philippines, they explored many islands including the island of [Mactan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mactan). However, Magellan was killed during the [Battle of Mactan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Mactan) against the local datu, [Lapulapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapulapu).[^cite_note-\:4-19][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-lac47-193] When Christian Spaniards went to the Philippines they even encountered Muslim speakers of Spanish who learned it from citizens of the former Islamic Spanish [Emirate of Granada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Granada), thus connecting both Western and Eastern extremes of the Muslim world.[^cite_note-194]

Over the next several decades, other Spanish expeditions were dispatched to the islands. [Ruy López de Villalobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruy_L%C3%B3pez_de_Villalobos) led an expedition that visited Leyte and Samar in 1543 and named them *Las Islas Filipinas* in honor of Philip of Asturias, the [Prince of Asturias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_of_Asturias) at the time.[^cite_note-195] Philip became [Philip II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain) on January 16, 1556, when his father, Charles I of Spain (who also reigned as [Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor)), abdicated the Spanish throne. The name was then extended to the entire archipelago later on in the Spanish era.



1734 Spanish Chart of the Philippine Islands

European colonialization began in earnest when Spanish explorer [Miguel López de Legazpi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_L%C3%B3pez_de_Legazpi) arrived from Mexico in 1565 and formed the first European settlements in Cebu. Side by side with these European settlements were towns founded by Native American [Tlaxcalans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tlaxcala_\(Nahua_state\)) who accompanied [Juan de Salcedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo), a knight born from Mexico, from Cebu and were given pensions, and they numbered 400 in the initial expedition.[^cite_note-196] Beginning with just five ships and five hundred men accompanied by Augustinian monks, and further strengthened in 1567 by two hundred soldiers, he was able to repel the Portuguese and create the foundations for the unification and colonialization of the Archipelago.[^cite_note-197] In 1571, the Spanish, their Latin-American recruits and their Filipino (Visayan) allies, commanded by able conquistadors such as Mexico-born [Juan de Salcedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo) (who was in love with Tondo's princess, [Kandarapa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kandarapa), a romance his Spanish grandfather Miguel Lopez de Legaspi, disapproved of) attacked [Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Maynila), a vassal-state of the Brunei Sultanate and liberated plus incorporated the [kingdom of Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tondo) as well as establishing [Manila](184334.md) as the capital of the [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKurlansky199964-198][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJoaquin1988-199][^cite_note-200] During the early part of the Spanish colonialization of the Philippines, the Spanish Augustinian friar Gaspar de San Agustín, O.S.A., describes Iloilo and Panay as one of the most populated islands in the archipelago and the most fertile of all the islands of the Philippines. He also talks about Iloilo, particularly the ancient settlement of Halaur, as site of a progressive trading post and a court of illustrious nobles.[^cite_note-201]

Legazpi built a fort in Maynila and made overtures of friendship to [Lakan Dula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakan_Dula), Lakan of Tondo, who accepted. However, Maynila's former ruler, the Muslim rajah, [Rajah Sulayman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Sulayman), who was a vassal to the Sultan of Brunei, refused to submit to Legazpi, but failed to get the support of Lakan Dula or of the Pampangan and Pangasinan settlements to the north. When [Tarik Sulayman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarik_Sulayman), alongside a force of Kapampangan and Tagalog Muslim warriors, attacked the Spaniards in the [battle of Bangkusay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bangkusay_Channel), he was finally defeated and killed. The Spanish also destroyed the walled Kapampangan city-state of [Cainta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cainta_\(historical_polity\)).



A late 17th-century manuscript by Gaspar de San Agustin from the [Archive of the Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archive_of_the_Indies), depicting López de Legazpi's conquest of the Philippines

In 1578, the [Castilian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castilian_War) erupted between the Christian Spaniards and Muslim Bruneians over control of the Philippine archipelago. On one side, the newly Christianized non-Muslim Visayans of Panay and [Cebu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebu_\(historical_polity\)), as well as [Butuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butuan_\(historical_polity\)) (which were from northern Mindanao), as well as the remnants of Bo-ol ([Dapitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dapitan_Kingdom)) had previously waged war against the [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu), [Sultanate of Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Maguindanao) and [Kingdom of Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maynila_\(historical_polity\)), then joined the Spanish in the war against the [Bruneian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruneian_Empire) and its allies, the Bruneian puppet-state of Maynila, Sulu which had dynastic links with Brunei as well as Maguindanao which was an ally of Sulu. The Spanish and its Visayan allies assaulted Brunei and seized its capital, [Kota Batu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kota_Batu,_Brunei-Muara). This was achieved as a result in part of the assistance rendered to them by two [noblemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pengiran#Brunei), Pengiran Seri Lela and Pengiran Seri Ratna. The former had traveled to Manila to offer Brunei as a [tributary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal_kingdom) of Spain for help to recover the throne usurped by his brother, Saiful Rijal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlip1964201,317-202] The Spanish agreed that if they succeeded in conquering Brunei, Pengiran Seri Lela would indeed become the sultan, while Pengiran Seri Ratna would be the new [Bendahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bendahara). In March 1578, the Spanish fleet, led by De Sande himself, acting as [Capitán General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_general), started their journey towards Brunei. The expedition consisted of 400 Spaniards and Mexicans, 1,500 [Filipino](2298105.md) natives and 300 Borneans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnnual_report_of_the_Secretary_of_War1903\[httpsbabelhathitrustorgcgiptidnjp32101050737699view1upseq393_379\]-203] The campaign was one of many, which also included action in [Mindanao](20546.md) and [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu_Archipelago).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcAmis200233-204][^cite_note-205]

The Spanish succeeded in invading the capital on April 16, 1578, with the help of Pengiran Seri Lela and Pengiran Seri Ratna. Sultan Saiful Rijal and Paduka Seri Begawan Sultan Abdul Kahar were forced to flee to Meragang then to [Jerudong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerudong). In Jerudong, they made plans to chase the conquering army away from Brunei. The Spanish suffered heavy losses due to a [cholera](7591.md) or [dysentery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysentery) outbreak.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrankham2008278-206][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAtiyah200271-207] They were so weakened by the illness that they decided to abandon Brunei to return to Manila on June 26, 1578, after just 72 days. Before doing so, they burned the mosque, a high structure with a five-tier roof.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaunders200254–60-208]

Pengiran Seri Lela died in August–September 1578, probably from the same illness that had afflicted his Spanish allies, although there was suspicion, he could have been poisoned by the ruling sultan. Seri Lela's daughter, the Bruneian princess, left with the Spanish and went on to marry a Christian [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_people), named Agustín de Legazpi of Tondo and had children in the Philippines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaunders200257-209]



Filipinos during the Spanish era.

Concurrently, northern Luzon became a center of the "Bahan Trade" (comercio de bafan), found in Luís Fróis' Historia de Japam, mainly refers to the robberies, raids, and pillages conducted by the Japanese pirates of Kyūshūa as they assaulted the China seas. The Sengoku period (1477–1603) or the warring states period of Japan had spread the [wakō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wokou)'s 倭寇 (Japanese Pirates) activities in the China Seas, some groups of these raiders relocated to the Philippines and established their settlements in Luzon. Because of the proximity to China's beaches, the Philippines were favorable a location to launch raids on the provinces of Guangdong and Fujian, and for shipping with Indochina and the Ryūkyū Islands.[^cite_note-210] These were the halcyon days of the Philippine branch of the Bahan trade. Thus, the Spanish sought to fight off these Japanese Pirates, prominent among whom was warlord Tayfusa,[^cite_note-211] whom the Spaniards expelled after he set up the beginnings of a city-state of Japanese pirates in Northern Luzon.[^cite_note-212] The Spanish repelled them in the fabled [1582 Cagayan battles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1582_Cagayan_battles).[^cite_note-213] Due to the 1549 Ming ban on trade leveled against the [Ashikaga shogunate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashikaga_shogunate) as a consequence of the Wokou pirate raids, this resulted in the ban for all the Japanese to enter China, and for Chinese ships to sail to Japan. Thus, Manila became the only place where the Japanese and Chinese can openly trade, often also trading Japanese silver for Chinese silk.[^cite_note-214]

In 1587, [Magat Salamat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magat_Salamat), one of the children of Lakandula, along with Lakandula's nephew and lords of the neighboring areas of Tondo, Pandacan, Marikina, Candaba, Navotas, and Bulacan, were executed when the [Tondo Conspiracy of 1587–1588](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_of_the_Maharlikas) failed[^cite_note-Tondo-215] in which a planned grand alliance with the Japanese Christian-captain, Gayo, (Gayo himself was a Woku who once pirated in Cagayan) and Brunei's sultan, would have restored the old aristocracy. Its failure resulted in the hanging of Agustín de Legaspi and the execution of Magat Salamat (the crown-prince of Tondo).[^cite_note-216] Thereafter, some of the conspirators were exiled to Guam or Guerrero, Mexico.

Spanish power was further consolidated after Miguel López de Legazpi's complete assimilation of Madja-as, his subjugation of [Rajah Tupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Tupas), the Rajah of Cebu and [Juan de Salcedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo)'s conquest of the provinces of Zambales, La Union, Ilocos, the coast of Cagayan, and the ransacking of the Chinese warlord [Limahong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limahong)'s pirate kingdom in [Pangasinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaboloan).[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-JoaquinPhilBecoming-218]

The Spanish also invaded [Northern Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Formosa) and [Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternate) in Indonesia, using Filipino warriors, before they were driven out by the Dutch.[^cite_note-219] The Sultanate of Ternate reverted to independence and afterwards led a coalition of sultanates against Spain.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] While Taiwan became the stronghold of the Ming-loyalist and pirate state of the [Kingdom of Tungning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tungning). The Spanish and the Moros of the sultanates of Maguindanao, Lanao, and Sulu also waged many wars over hundreds of years in the [Spanish–Moro conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93Moro_conflict), they were supported by the Papuan language speaking [Sultanate of Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ternate) in Indonesia which regained independence from Spain,[^cite_note-222] as well as the Sultanate of Brunei, not until the 19th century did Spain succeed in defeating the Sulu Sultanate and taking Mindanao under nominal suzerainty.

> Persians and Arabs and Egyptians and Turks brought \[Muhammad's] veneration and evil sect here, and even Moors from Tunis and Granada came here, sometimes in the armadas of Campson \[Kait Bey], former Sultan of Cairo and King of Egypt... Thus it seems to me that these Moros of the Philippine Islands \[are] mainly those who, as had been said, come from Egypt and Arabia and Mecca, and are their relatives, disciples and members, and every year they say that Turks come to Sumatra and Borneo, and to Ternate, where there are now some of those defeated in the famous battle which Señor Don Juan de Austria won.[^cite_note-223]

— Melchor Davalos

The Spanish considered their war with the Muslims in Southeast Asia an extension of the [Reconquista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconquista), a centuries-long campaign to retake and rechristianize the Spanish homeland which was invaded by the Muslims of the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate). The Spanish expeditions into the Philippines were also part of a larger Ibero-Islamic world conflict[^cite_note-224] that included a war [against the Ottoman Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Habsburg_wars) which had just invaded former Christian lands in the Eastern Mediterranean and which had a center of operations in Southeast Asia at its nearby vassal, the [Sultanate of Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_expedition_to_Aceh).[^cite_note-225] Thus the Philippines became a theatre of the ongoing world-wide-ranging [Ottoman–Habsburg wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Habsburg_wars).

In time, Spanish fortifications were also set up in [Taiwan](25734.md) and the [Maluku islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_islands). These were abandoned and the Spanish soldiers, along with the [newly Christianized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ternate) natives of the [Moluccas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moluccas), withdrew back to the Philippines in order to re-concentrate their military forces because of a threatened invasion by the Japan-born [Ming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming)-dynasty loyalist, [Koxinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koxinga), ruler of the [Kingdom of Tungning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tungning).[^cite_note-226] However, the planned invasion was aborted. Meanwhile, settlers were sent to the Pacific islands of [Palau](23372.md) and the [Marianas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Islands).[^cite_note-227]

To counteract the Muslim threats reinforced from [Ottoman Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkey), the Spaniards imported Latin American soldiers from [Peru](170691.md), [Panama](22997.md), and [Mexico](3966054.md).[^cite_note-Diaz1635-228]

> "He (Governor Don Sebastían Hurtado de Corcuera) brought a great reënforcements of soldiers, many of them from Perú, as he made his voyage to Acapulco from that kingdom."[^cite_note-Diaz1635-228]

— Casimiro Díaz



The sketch of the [Plaza de Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_de_Roma) Manila by Fernando Brambila, a member of the Malaspina Expedition during their stop in Manila in 1792.

In 1593, a diplomatic entourage addressed to the "King of Luzon" from the King of Cambodia which bore an elephant as a tribute[^cite_note-229] arrived in Manila. The King of Cambodia which witnessed the military activity of precolonial [Luzones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzones) people who were mercenaries across Southeast Asia including at Burma and Siam,[^cite_note-230] now implored the new rulers of Luzon, the Spaniards, to aid him in a war to retake his kingdom from an invasion by the Siamese.[^cite_note-Kohn2013-231] That had caused the ill-fated [Spanish expedition to Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Spanish_War) that although ended in failure had set the foundations of the future restoration of Cambodia from Thai rule under [French Cochinchina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Cochinchina) which tapped Spanish allies.

### Incorporation to the Mexico-based Viceroyalty of New Spain



Map of the Mexico Centered Viceroyalty of New Spain as of year 1794. Due to distance from [Spain](26667.md) proper: colonists, soldiers, and the subsidy for the Philippines was organized from nearer [Mexico](3966054.md).

The founding of [Manila](184334.md) by uniting the dominions of [Sulayman III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Sulayman) and [Rajah Ache *Matanda*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajah_Matanda) of [Maynila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Maynila) who was a vassal to the Sultan of Brunei, and [Lakandula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakandula) of [Tondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tondo_\(historical_polity\)) who paid tribute to [Ming dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ming_dynasty) China – caused the creation of Manila on February 6, 1579, through the [Papal bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_bull) *Illius Fulti Præsidio* by [Pope Gregory XIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XIII), encompassing all [Spanish colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain) in Asia as a [suffragan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffragan_diocese) of the [Archdiocese of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Mexico).[^cite_note-232] Aside from Manila the capital, the Spanish and Latino populations were first concentrated in the 5 newly founded Spanish Royal Cities of [Cebu](285868.md), [Arevalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iloilo_City), [Nueva Segovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal-lo), [Nueva Caceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naga,_Camarines_Sur), and [Vigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigan_City).[^cite_note-233] Aside from these cities, they were also scattered across the [Presidios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidios) of [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite_City), [Calamianes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamianes), [Caraga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caraga_Region), and [Zamboanga](25167165.md).[^cite_note-234] For much of the Spanish period, the Philippines was part of the Mexico-based [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Spain). Of the Spaniards and Latinos sent to the Philippines, almost half of the individuals levied to Manila were reported in judicial files as españoles (Spanish born in the colonies, who were often just "very pale [mestizos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizos)"), and about a third, as mestizos (whereas Indian ([Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_people)), [mulattos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulatto), and blacks could be mistaken for mestizos of darker color).[^cite_note-235] Castizos amounted to a total of 15 percent, while peninsulares (Spaniards born in Spain) were around 5 percent of those punished with deportation to Manila.[^cite_note-236]

The Spanish rationalized the demographic layout, resource extraction, defense, and commerce of the colony; which was previously a scattered constellation of rival precolonial kingdoms, via a process known as the "Iberian [Reductions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reductions)", wherein people were relocated, sometimes forcibly, from their previous settlements and were resettled into new towns and barrios which were modeled after academic standards formed in Spain and Portugal.[^cite_note-Real_Academia-237] People were moved into a centralized *[cabecera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipio)* (town/district capital), where a newly built church and an *[ayuntamiento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayuntamiento)* (town hall) were situated.[^cite_note-ThePhilAPastRev-238] This allowed the government to defend, control, and [Christianize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization) the indigenous population in scattered independent settlements, to conduct [population counts](6889.md), and to collect [tributes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax).[^cite_note-239] The new aristocracy in these newly founded towns were the [Principalía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal%C3%ADa), which arose when [Spanish and Mexican settlers intermixed with the precolonial native royalty and Chinese merchant elites, and formed a nascent landed gentry per each locale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Encomederos_and_Nobilities).[^cite_note-gaceta-240]<sup>: p1 cols 1–4 </sup>

### Spanish settlement during the 16th and 17th centuries



Spanish era Manila canal

The "Memoria de las Encomiendas en las Islas" of 1591, just twenty years after the conquest of Luzon, reveals a remarkable progress in the work of colonialization and the spread of Christianity.[^cite_note-241] A cathedral was built in the city of Manila with an episcopal palace, Augustinian, Dominican, and Franciscan monasteries and a Jesuit house. The king maintained a hospital for the Spanish settlers and there was another hospital for the natives run by the Franciscans. In order to defend the settlements the Spaniards established in the Philippines, a network of military fortresses called "[Presidios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidio)" were constructed and officered by the Spaniards, and sentried by Latin-Americans and Filipinos, across the archipelago, to protect it from foreign nations such as the Portuguese, British, and Dutch as well as raiding Muslims and [Wokou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wokou).[^cite_note-242]

The Manila garrison was composed of roughly four hundred Spanish soldiers and the area of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros) as well as its surroundings, were initially settled by 1200 Spanish families.[^cite_note-243] In [Cebu City](285868.md), at the Visayas, the settlement received a total of 2,100 soldier-settlers from [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain).[^cite_note-244] At the immediate south of Manila, Mexicans were present at Ermita[^cite_note-245] and at [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite_City)[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-autogenerated235-247][^cite_note-248] where they were stationed as sentries. In addition, men conscripted from [Peru](170691.md), were also sent to settle [Zamboanga City](25167165.md) in Mindanao, to wage war upon Muslim pirates.[^cite_note-249] These Peruvian soldiers who settled in Zamboanga were led by Don [Sebastián Hurtado de Corcuera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Hurtado_de_Corcuera) who was governor of [Panama](22997.md).[^cite_note-Peru-250] He also used Panamanians, including even some Genoese from [Panama Viejo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Viejo) descended from colonists at the [Republic of Genoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa), a nation once active in the [Crusades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades).[^cite_note-251] There were also communities of Spanish-Mestizos that developed in [Iloilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iloilo_City),[^cite_note-252] [Negros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negros_Island),[^cite_note-253] and [Vigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigan).[^cite_note-IlocosHeritage-254]



[Principalía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principal%C3%ADa) family by Simón Flores y de la Rosa, uncle of painter [Fabián de la Rosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabi%C3%A1n_de_la_Rosa)

Interactions between native Filipinos[^cite_note-filipinoterm-256] and immigrant Spaniards plus the Latin-Americans and their Spanish-Mestizo descendants eventually caused the formation of a new language, [Chavacano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavacano), a creole of [Mexican Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Spanish).[^cite_note-257] Meanwhile, in the suburb of Tondo, there was a convent run by Franciscan friars and another by the Dominicans that offered Christian education to the Chinese converts to Christianity. The same report reveals that in and around Manila were collected 9,410 tributes, indicating a population of about 30,640 who were under the instruction of thirteen missionaries (ministers of doctrine), apart from the monks in monasteries. In the former province of Pampanga the population estimate was 74,700 and 28 missionaries. In Pangasinan 2,400 people with eight missionaries. In Cagayan and islands Babuyanes 96,000 people but no missionaries. In La Laguna 48,400 people with 27 missionaries. In Bicol and Camarines Catanduanes islands 86,640 people with fifteen missionaries. Based on the tribute counts, the total founding population of Spanish-Philippines, as of year 1591, was 667,612 people,[^cite_note-258] of which: 20,000 were Chinese migrant traders,[^cite_note-259] 15,600 were Latino soldier-colonists sent from Peru and Mexico (In the 1600s),[^cite_note-260] Immigrants included 3,000 Japanese residents,[^cite_note-261] and 600 pure Spaniards from Europe.[^cite_note-262] Of the 600 Spaniards from Europe, two hundred and thirty-six of them were given [encomiendas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encomiendas) and were ennobled, as they scattered across the many provinces of the Philippines to serve as administrators.[^cite_note-263] There was also a large but unknown number of [Indian Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Filipinos) as majority of the slaves imported into the archipelago were from [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) or Southern [India](14533.md),[^cite_note-264] adding [Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_language) speaking South Indians and [Indo-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language) speaking [Bangladeshis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Bangladesh) into the ethnic mix, and the rest were Malays and Negritos. They were under the care of 140 missionaries, of which 79 were Augustinians, nine Dominicans, and 42 Franciscans.[^cite_note-265] Adding during the Spanish evacuation of [Ternate, Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternate,_Indonesia), the 200 families of mixed Mexican-Filipino-Spanish and Moluccan-Portuguese descent who had ruled over the briefly Christianized [Sultanate of Ternate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Ternate) (They later reverted to Islam) were relocated to [Ternate, Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ternate,_Cavite) and Ermita, Manila.[^cite_note-266] and they were presaged by their previous ruler, Sultan Said Din Burkat who was enslaved but eventually converted to Christianity and was freed after being deported to [Manila](184334.md).[^cite_note-267]



A *[Gobernadorcillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobernadorcillo)* de Naturales comparable to a modern-day mayor. Mostly of Indio descent.

The islands were fragmented and [sparsely populated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Philippines#Population_history)[^cite_note-268] due to constant inter-kingdom wars[^cite_note-269] and natural disasters (as the country is on the [Typhoon belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon#Frequency) and [Pacific Ring of Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ring_of_Fire)),[^cite_note-jstor.org-191] which made it easy for the Spanish invasion. The Spanish then brought political unification to most of the Philippine archipelago via the conquest of the various small maritime states although they were unable to fully incorporate parts of the [sultanates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanates) of [Mindanao](20546.md) and the areas where the ethnic groups and highland plutocracy of the animist [Ifugao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifugao) of Northern [Luzon](69375.md) were established. The Spanish introduced elements of [western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) such as the [code of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_law), western printing, and the [Gregorian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_calendar) alongside new food resources such as maize, [pineapple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple), and chocolate from Latin America.[^cite_note-270]

[Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_education_during_Spanish_rule) played a major role in the socio-economic transformation of the archipelago. The oldest universities, [colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College), and [vocational schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_schools) and the first modern [public education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school) system in Asia were all created during the Spanish colonial period, and by the time Spain was replaced by the United States as the colonial power, Filipinos were among the most educated subjects in all of Asia.[^cite_note-271] The Jesuits founded the Colegio de Manila in 1590, which later became the [Universidad de San Ignacio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universidad_de_San_Ignacio), a royal and pontifical university. They also founded the [Colegio de San Ildefonso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colegio_de_San_Ildefonso) on August 1, 1595. After the [expulsion of the Society of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suppression_of_the_Society_of_Jesus#Philippines) in 1768, the management of the Jesuit schools passed to other parties. On April 28, 1611, through the initiative of Bishop Miguel de Benavides, the [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas) was founded in Manila. The Jesuits also founded the Colegio de San José (1601) and took over the Escuela Municipal, later to be called the [Ateneo de Manila University](330339.md) (1859). All institutions offered courses included not only religious topics but also [Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_the_Philippines) subjects such as physics, chemistry, natural history, and mathematics. The University of Santo Tomás, for example, started by teaching theology, philosophy and humanities, and the Faculty of Jurisprudence and Canonical Law, together with the schools of medicine and pharmacy were opened during the 18th century.



*[Bahay na bato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahay_na_bato)*, a typical Filipino urban house during the colonial era

Outside the tertiary institutions, the efforts of missionaries were in no way limited to religious instruction but also geared towards promoting social and economic advancement of the islands. They cultivated into the natives their taste for music and taught Spanish language to children.[^cite_note-272] They also introduced advances in rice agriculture, brought maize and cocoa from America, and developed the farming of indigo, coffee, and sugar cane. The only commercial plant introduced by a government agency was the plant of tobacco.

Church and state were inseparably linked in Spanish policy, with the state assuming responsibility for religious establishments.[^cite_note-uslc-4-273] One of Spain's objectives in colonialization of the Philippines was the conversion of the local population to Christianity. The work of conversion was facilitated by the disunity and insignificance of other organized religions, except for Islam, which was still predominant in the southwest.[^cite_note-uslc-4-273] The pageantry of the church had a wide appeal, reinforced by the incorporation of indigenous social customs into religious observances. The eventual outcome was a new Christian majority, from which the Muslims of western Mindanao and the upland tribal and animistic peoples of Luzon remained detached and alienated (Ethnic groups such as the Ifugaos of the Cordillera region and the Mangyans of Mindoro).



Villa Fernandina de [Vigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigan) founded by the Mexican conquistador [Juan de Salcedo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Salcedo).

At the lower levels of administration, the Spanish built on traditional village organization by co-opting local leaders. This system of indirect rule helped create an indigenous upper class, called the *principalía*, who had local wealth, high status, and other privileges. This perpetuated an [oligarchic](22315.md) system of local control. Among the most significant changes under Spanish rule was that the indigenous idea of communal use and ownership of land was replaced with the concept of private ownership and the conferring of titles on members of the *principalía*.[^cite_note-uslc-4-273]

Around 1608 [William Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Adams_\(sailor,_born_1564\)), an English navigator, contacted the interim governor of the Philippines, [Rodrigo de Vivero y Velasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_de_Vivero_y_Velasco), on behalf of [Tokugawa Ieyasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokugawa_Ieyasu), who wished to establish direct trade contacts with [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain). Friendly letters were exchanged, officially starting relations between Japan and New Spain. From 1565 to 1821, the Philippines was governed as a territory of the [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Spain) from Mexico, via the *Royal [Audiencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audiencia_Real)* of Manila, and administered directly from Spain from 1821 after the [Mexican revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_War_of_Independence),[^cite_note-274] until 1898.

The [Manila galleons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_galleons), were constructed in [Bicol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicol_Region) and [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite).[^cite_note-275] The Manila galleons were accompanied with a large naval escort as it traveled to and from Manila and [Acapulco](1446.md).[^cite_note-276] The galleons sailed once or twice a year, between the 16th and 19th centuries.[^cite_note-277] The Manila Galleons brought with them goods,[^cite_note-278] settlers[^cite_note-autogenerated235-247], and military reinforcements[^cite_note-279] destined for the Philippines, from [Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_Asian).[^cite_note-gutenberg1-280] The reverse voyage also brought Asian commercial products[^cite_note-281] and [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Mexicans)[^cite_note-282] to the western side of the Americas.[^cite_note-283] Legally, the Manila Galleons were only allowed to trade between Mexico and the Philippines; however, illegal trade, commerce, and inter-migration, were happening in secret between the Philippines and other would-be nations in the Spanish Americas due to the tremendous demand and profitability of Asian products in Latin America. This clandestine defiance of Spanish colonial decrees forbidding trade continued all throughout the term of the [Manila Galleons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Galleons).[^cite_note-284]

The Spanish military fought off various indigenous revolts and several external challenges, especially from the British, Dutch, and Portuguese and Chinese pirates. Christian missionaries converted most of the lowland inhabitants to Christianity and founded schools, universities, and hospitals. In 1863 a Spanish decree introduced education, establishing public schooling in Spanish.[^cite_note-286]

In 1646, a series of five naval actions known as the [Battles of La Naval de Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battles_of_La_Naval_de_Manila) was fought between the forces of Spain and the [Dutch Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Republic), as part of the [Eighty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighty_Years'_War). Although the Spanish forces consisted of just two Manila galleons and a [galley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galley) with crews composed mainly of Filipino volunteers, against three separate Dutch squadrons, totaling eighteen ships, the Dutch squadrons were severely defeated in all fronts by the Spanish-Filipino forces, forcing the Dutch to abandon their plans for an invasion of the Philippines.

In 1687, [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton) included an explicit reference to the Philippines in his classic [Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophi%C3%A6_Naturalis_Principia_Mathematica) by mentioning Leuconia, the ancient Ptolemaic name for the Philippines.[^cite_note-manapat-71]

### Spanish rule during the 18th century



[Coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms) of Manila were at the corners of the [Cross of Burgundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_of_Burgundy_flag) in the Spanish-Filipino battle standard.

Colonial income derived mainly from [entrepôt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrep%C3%B4t) trade: The [Manila Galleons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Galleon) sailing from the port of Manila to the port of Acapulco on the west coast of Mexico brought shipments of [silver bullion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_bullion), and minted coin that were exchanged for return cargoes of Asian, and Pacific products. A total of 110 Manila galleons set sail in the 250 years of the Manila-Acapulco galleon trade (1565 to 1815). There was no direct trade with Spain until 1766.[^cite_note-uslc-4-273]



Plaza Santo Tomas in Intramuros, Manila; where the [Santo Domingo Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_Domingo_Church), [Colegio de Santa Rosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colegio_de_Santa_Rosa_-_Intramuros) and the original [University of Santo Tomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Santo_Tomas) were built during the Spanish era.

The Philippines was never profitable as a colony during Spanish rule, and the long war against the [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_Empire) from the West, in the 17th century together with the intermittent conflict with the Muslims in the South and combating Japanese [Wokou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wokou) piracy from the North nearly bankrupted the colonial treasury.[^cite_note-uslc-4-273] Furthermore, the state of near constant war caused a high death and desertion rate among the [Mestizo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mestizo) and Indio ([Native American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas)) soldiers[^cite_note-gutenberg1-280] sent from Mexico and Peru that were stationed in the Philippines.[^cite_note-287] The high death and desertion rate also applied to the native Filipino[^cite_note-filipinoterm-256] warriors conscripted by Spain, to fight in battles all across the archipelago. The repeated wars, lack of wages, and near starvation were so intense, almost half of the soldiers sent from Latin America either died or fled to the countryside to live as vagabonds among the rebellious natives or escaped enslaved Indians (from India)[^cite_note-288] where they race-mixed through rape or prostitution, further blurring the racial caste system Spain tried hard to maintain.[^cite_note-289] Mixed Spanish-Filipino descent may be more common than expected as many Spaniards often had Filipino concubines and mistresses, and they frequently produced children out of wedlock.[^cite_note-290] These circumstances contributed to the increasing difficulty of governing the Philippines. The Royal Fiscal of Manila wrote a letter to [King Charles III of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III_of_Spain) in which he advises to abandon the colony, but the religious orders opposed this since they considered the Philippines a launching pad for the conversion of the Far East.[^cite_note-Newson-291]<sup>: [7–8](https://books.google.com/books?id=A40BEAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA7) </sup>[^cite_note-292]

The Philippines survived on an annual subsidy paid by the Spanish Crown and often procured from taxes and profits accrued by the Viceroyalty of New Spain (Mexico), and the 200-year-old fortifications at Manila had not been improved much since first built by the Spanish.[^cite_note-tracy1995p12p55-293] This was one of the circumstances that made possible the brief British occupation of Manila between 1762 and 1764.

#### British occupation (1762–1764)

Main article: [British occupation of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_occupation_of_Manila)



"Allegory of the Defense of the Philippines" honors Don Simón de Anda y Salazar, who resisted the British invasion during the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War). Although Manila fell to George III’s superior forces in 1762, Anda y Salazar’s persistent counter-siege confined the British to the capital and prevented a total takeover. Ultimately, the British were forced to withdraw in defeat.

Britain declared war against Spain on January 4, 1762, and on September 24, 1762, a force of British Army regulars and [British East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) soldiers, supported by the ships and men of the East Indies Squadron of the British [Royal Navy](26061.md), sailed into [Manila Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Bay) from Madras, India.[^cite_note-tracy1995p9-294] [Manila was besieged and fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1762\)) to the British on October 4, 1762.

Outside of Manila, the Spanish leader [Simón de Anda y Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_de_Anda_y_Salazar) organized a militia of 10,000 mostly from [Pampanga](255602.md) to resist British attempts to extend their conquest outside Manila. Anda y Salazar established his headquarters first in Bulacan, then in Bacolor.[^cite_note-tracy1995p58-295] After a number of skirmishes and failed attempts to support Filipino uprisings, the British command admitted to the War Secretary in London that the Spanish were "in full possession of the country".[^cite_note-296] The occupation of Manila ended in April 1764 as agreed to in the peace negotiations for the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War) in Europe. The Spanish then persecuted the [Binondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binondo) Chinese community for its role in aiding the British.[^cite_note-en.radio86.com-297] An unknown number of [Indian soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_armies) known as [sepoys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepoy), who came with the British, deserted and settled in nearby [Cainta, Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cainta,_Rizal), which explains the uniquely Indian features of generations of Cainta residents.[^cite_note-298]

#### Spanish rule in the second part of the 18th century

In 1766 direct communication was established with Spain and trade with Europe through a national ship based on Spain. In 1774, colonial officers from Bulacan, Tondo, Laguna de Bay, and other areas surrounding Manila reported with consternation that discharged soldiers and deserters (from Mexico, Spain and Peru) during the British occupation were providing the indios military training for the weapons that had been disseminated all over the territory during the war.[^cite_note-299] Expeditions from Spain were administered since 1785 by the [Real Compañía de Filipinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Compa%C3%B1%C3%ADa_de_Filipinas), which was granted a monopoly of trade between Spain and the islands that lasted until 1834, when the company was terminated by the Spanish crown due to poor management and financial losses.[^cite_note-300] About this time, Governor-General Anda complained that the Latin-American and Spanish soldiers sent to the Philippines had dispersed "all over the islands, even the most distant, looking for subsistence".[^cite_note-301]

In 1780, direct trade between [Manila](184334.md), [Philippines](23440.md) at Asia; and Callao, [Peru](170691.md); in South America, was officially approved by the Spanish King, and organized by the Royal Philippine Company, leading to much immigration of Filipinos to Peru and Peruvians to the Philippines.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] In 1781, Governor-General [José Basco y Vargas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Basco_y_Vargas) established the [Economic Society of the Friends of the Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociedad_Econ%C3%B3mica_de_los_Amigos_del_Pa%C3%ADs).[^cite_note-uslc-5b-304] The Philippines was administered from the [Viceroyalty of New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroyalty_of_New_Spain) until the independence to Mexico in 1821 necessitated the direct rule from Spain of the Philippines from that year.

In the late 1700s to early 1800s, Joaquín Martínez de Zúñiga, an Agustinian Friar from Spain, in his Two Volume Book: "Estadismo de las islas Filipinas"[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305][^cite_note-Estadismo2-306] compiled a census of the Spanish-Philippines based on the tribute counts (Which represented an average family of seven to ten children[^cite_note-307] and two parents, per tribute)[^cite_note-Newson-291] and came upon the following statistics:

The Spanish-Filipino population as a proportion of the provinces widely varied; with as high as 19% of the population of Tondo province[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> (The most populous province and former name of Manila), to Pampanga 13.7%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Cavite at 13%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Laguna 2.28%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Batangas 3%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Bulacan 10.79%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Bataan 16.72%,[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Ilocos 1.38%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 31 </sup> Pangasinan 3.49%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 31 </sup> Albay 1.16%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 54 </sup> Cebu 2.17%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 113 </sup> Samar 3.27%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 113 </sup> Iloilo 1%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 113 </sup> Capiz 1%,[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 113 </sup> [Bicol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicol_region) 20%,[^cite_note-Pnas-309] and [Zamboanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamboanga_Peninsula) 40%.[^cite_note-Pnas-309] According to the data, in the Archdiocese of Manila which administers much of Luzon under it, about 10% of the population was Spanish-Filipino.[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup> Summing up all the provinces including those with no Spanish Filipinos, all in all, in the total population of the Philippines, [Spanish Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Filipinos) and mixed Spanish-Filipinos composed 5% of the population.[^cite_note-Estadismo1-305]<sup>: 539 </sup>[^cite_note-Estadismo2-306]<sup>: 31, 54, 113 </sup>

The book, "Intercolonial Intimacies Relinking Latin/o America to the Philippines, 1898–1964 By Paula C. Park" citing "Forzados y reclutas: los criollos novohispanos en Asia (1756-1808)" gave a higher number of later Mexican soldier-immigrants to the Philippines, pegging the number at 35,000 immigrants in the 1700s,[^cite_note-Intercolonial-310] in a Philippine population which was only around 1.5 Million,[^cite_note-311] thus forming 2.33% of the population.[^cite_note-312]

Meanwhile, government records show that 20% of the Philippines' total population were either pure Chinese or Mixed [Chinese-Filipinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese-Filipinos)[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-senate.gov.ph-314]

### Spanish rule during the 19th century



The landing of the [Spanish expedition to Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_expedition_to_Balanguingui) by [Antonio Brugada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Brugada).

The Philippines was included in the vast territory of the Kingdom of Spain, in the first constitution of Spain promulgated in Cadiz in 1812. It was never a colony as modern-day historical literature would say, but an overseas region in Asia (Spanish Constitution 1812). The Spanish Constitution of 1870 provides for the first autonomous community for "Archipelago Filipino" where all provinces in the Philippine Islands will be given the semi-independent home rule program.

In 1818; the general Native, Chinese-Filipino, and Spanish-Filipino, populations of the Philippines ballooned, and Buzeta and Bravo recorded these numbers in their general census.

Filipina mestiza women

During the 19th century Spain invested heavily in education and infrastructure. Through the Education Decree of December 20, 1863, [Queen Isabella II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_II_of_Spain) decreed the establishment of a free public school system that used Spanish as the language of instruction, leading to increasing numbers of educated Filipinos.[^cite_note-319] Additionally, the opening of the [Suez Canal](29323.md) in 1869 cut travel time to Spain, which facilitated the rise of the [ilustrados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilustrado), an enlightened class of Spanish-Filipinos that had been able to enroll in Spanish and European universities.

A great number of infrastructure projects were undertaken during the 19th century that put the Philippine economy and standard of living ahead of most of its Asian neighbors and even many European countries at that time. Among them were a [railway system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_National_Railways) for Luzon, a tramcar network for Manila, and Asia's first steel suspension bridge Puente Claveria, later called [Puente Colgante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puente_Colgante_\(Manila\)).

[^cite_note-Borja-320]



*[Ilustrados](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilustrado)* in Madrid, c. 1890

On August 1, 1851, the [Banco Español-Filipino de Isabel II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_the_Philippine_Islands) was established to attend the needs of the rapid economic boom, that had greatly increased its pace since the 1800s as a result of a new economy based on a rational exploitation of the agricultural resources of the islands. The increase in textile fiber crops such as [abacá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abac%C3%A1), oil products derived from the coconut, indigo, that was growing in demand, etc., generated an increase in money supply that led to the creation of the bank. Banco Español-Filipino was also granted the power to print a Philippine-specific currency (the [Philippine peso](227672.md)) for the first time (before 1851, many currencies were used, mostly the [pieces of eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieces_of_eight)).



Filipino [Marcelo Azcárraga Palmero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Azc%C3%A1rraga_Palmero) born in Manila to a [Vizcayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biscay) Spaniard who was a peninsulares general in the Philippines José de Azcárraga and a Filipina mestiza María Palmero. He became the Prime minister of Spain.

Spanish Manila was seen in the 19th century as a model of colonial governance that effectively put the interests of the original inhabitants of the islands before those of the colonial power. As [John Crawfurd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Crawfurd) put it in its History of the Indian Archipelago, in all of Asia the "Philippines alone did improve in civilization, wealth, and populousness under the colonial rule" of a foreign power.[^cite_note-321] [John Bowring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bowring), Governor General of British Hong Kong from 1856 to 1860, wrote after his trip to Manila:

> Credit is certainly due to Spain for having bettered the condition of a people who, though comparatively highly civilized, yet being continually distracted by petty wars, had sunk into a disordered and uncultivated state. The inhabitants of these beautiful Islands upon the whole, may well be considered to have lived as comfortably during the last hundred years, protected from all external enemies and governed by mild laws vis-a-vis those from any other tropical country under native or European sway, owing in some measure, to the frequently discussed peculiar (Spanish) circumstances which protect the interests of the natives.[^cite_note-322]

In *The Inhabitants of the Philippines*, Frederick Henry Sawyer wrote:

> Until an inept bureaucracy was substituted for the old paternal rule, and the revenue quadrupled by increased taxation, the Filipinos were as happy a community as could be found in any colony. The population greatly multiplied; they lived in competence, if not in affluence; cultivation was extended, and the exports steadily increased. \[...] Let us be just; what British, French, or Dutch colony, populated by natives can compare with the Philippines as they were until 1895?.[^cite_note-323]

The first official census in the Philippines was carried out in 1878. The colony's population as of December 31, 1877, was recorded at 5,567,685 persons.[^cite_note-324] This was followed by the 1887 census that yielded a count of 6,984,727,[^cite_note-Gonzalez93-325] while that of 1898 yielded 7,832,719 inhabitants.[^cite_note-326]

### Latin-American revolutions and direct Spanish rule

Main article: [Philippine Military Activities in the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Military_Activities_in_the_Americas)



Filipino Mestizo priests [Mariano Gomez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Gomez_\(priest\)), [José Burgos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Burgos), and [Jacinto Zamora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacinto_Zamora) collectively known as the [Gomburza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gomburza) was wrongly executed after [1872 Cavite mutiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1872_Cavite_mutiny). It sparked the movements that would later bring about the revolution that would end Spain's control of the archipelago.&#x20;

In the Americas; overseas Filipinos were involved in several anti-colonial movements, Filomeno V. Aguilar Jr. in his paper: "Manilamen and seafaring: engaging the maritime world beyond the Spanish realm", stated therein that Filipinos who were internationally called Manilamen were active in the navies and armies of the world even after the era of the Manila Galleons such as the case of the [Argentine war of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argentine_war_of_independence) wherein an Argentinian of French descent, Hypolite Bouchard, laid siege to Monterey California as a privateer for the Argentine army. His second ship, the Santa Rosa, which was captained by the American Peter Corney, had a multi-ethnic crew which included Filipinos.[^cite_note-327] It has been proposed that those Filipinos were recruited in [San Blas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Blas,_Nayarit), an alternative port to Acapulco Mexico where several Filipinos had settled during the Manila-Acapulco Galleon trade era.[^cite_note-328] Argentinian-Philippine relations can be traced even earlier since the Philippines already received immigrants from South America, like the soldier [Juan Fermín de San Martín](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Ferm%C3%ADn_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn), who was the brother of the leader of the Argentinian Revolution [Jose de San Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_de_San_Martin). Likewise, in Mexico, about 200 Filipinos were recruited by [Miguel Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Hidalgo) in his revolution against Spain, the most prominent of which was the Manila-born [Ramon Fabié](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Fabi%C3%A9)[^cite_note-mexicanembassy-329][^cite_note-330] afterwards when the revolution was continued by President Guerrero, General [Isidoro Montes de Oca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isidoro_Montes_de_Oca), another Filipino-Mexican, had participated in the Mexican Revolutionary war against Spain too.[^cite_note-331] The recent participation of overseas Filipinos in Anti-Imperial wars in the Americas started even earlier when Filipinos in the settlement of [Saint Malo, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Malo,_Louisiana) assisted the United States in the defense of New Orleans during the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812).[^cite_note-AFPS-332]

Upon Mexican independence, the Filipinos had such an effect on Mexico that there were plans among the newly independent Mexicans to help the Filipinos revolt against Spain too. There was even a secret memorandum from the Mexican government which read:

> Now that we Mexicans have fortunately obtained our independence by revolution against Spanish rule, it is our solemn duty to help the less fortunate countries especially the Philippines, with whom our country has had the most intimate relations during the last two centuries and a half. We should send secret agents with a message to their inhabitants to rise in revolution against Spain and that we shall give them financial and military assistance to win their freedom. Should the Philippines succeed in gaining her independence from Spain, we must felicitate her warmly and from an alliance of amity and commerce with her as a sister nation. Moreover, we must resume the intimate Mexico-Philippine relations, as they were during the halcyon days of the Acapulco-Manila galleon trade.[^cite_note-333]

Likewise, in this period, overseas Filipinos were also active in the Asia-Pacific especially in China and Indochina. During the Taiping rebellion, Frederick Townsend Ward had a militia employing foreigners to quell the rebellion for the Qing government, at first he hired American and European adventurers but they proved unruly, while recruiting for better troops, he met his aide-de-camp, Vincente (Vicente?) Macanaya, who was twenty three years old in 1860 and was part of the large Filipino population then living in Shanghai, who "were handy on board ships and more than a little troublesome on land", as Caleb Carr journalistically put it.[^cite_note-334] Smith, another writer about China also notes in his book: "Mercenaries and Mandarins" that Manilamen were "Reputed to be brave and fierce fighters" and "were plentiful in Shanghai and always eager for action". During this Taiping rebellion, by July 1860, Townsend Ward's force of Manilamen ranging from one to two hundred mercenaries successfully assaulted Sung-Chiang Prefecture.[^cite_note-335] Thus, while the Philippines was slowly engendered with revolutionary fervour being suppressed by Spain, overseas Filipinos have had an active role in the military and naval engagements of various nations in the [Americas](29833.md) and [Asia-Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia-Pacific).[^cite_note-336] Soldiers from the Philippines were recruited by [France](5843419.md), which was allied to [Spain](26667.md), to initially protect Indo-Chinese Christian converts who were persecuted by their native governments, and later, for an actual conquest of Vietnam and Laos, as well as the establishment of the Protectorate of Cambodia which was liberated from Thai invasions and re-established as a vassal-state of France with the combined Franco-Spanish-Filipino forces, creating [French Cochinchina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Cochinchina) which was governed from the former Cambodian and now Vietnamese city of [Saigon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saigon).[^cite_note-nigelgooding.co.uk-337]



Santa Lucia Gate, [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros), [Manila overlooking](184334.md) [San Agustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Agustin_Church_\(Manila\)), [San Ignacio Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Ignacio_Church_\(Manila\)) belltowers and [Ateneo de Manila](330339.md) where Jose Rizal once studied.

The *_Criollo_* and *Latino* dissatisfaction against the Peninsulares (Spaniards direct from Spain), spurred by their love of the land and their suffering people, had a justified hatred against the exploitative *[Peninsulares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsulares)* who were only appointed to high positions due to their race and unflinching loyalty to the homeland. This resulted in the 1823 uprising of [Andres Novales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andres_Novales), a Philippine born soldier of Mexican descent,[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPark2022159-340] who earned great fame in richer Spain but chose to return to serve in poorer Philippines. He was supported by local soldiers as well as former officers in the Spanish army of the Philippines who were primarily from the now sovereign [Mexico](3966054.md)[^cite_note-341] as well as the freshly independent nations of [Colombia](5222.md), [Venezuela](32374.md), [Peru](170691.md), [Chile](5489.md), [Argentina](18951905.md) and [Costa Rica](5551.md).[^cite_note-342] The uprising was brutally suppressed, but it foreshadowed the 1872 [Cavite Mutiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite_Mutiny) that was a precursor to the Philippine Revolution.[^cite_note-Cavite_Mutiny-343][^cite_note-ReferenceB-344][^cite_note-RichardsonBonifacio-345] However, Hispanic-Philippines reached its zenith when the Philippine-born [Marcelo Azcárraga Palmero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Azc%C3%A1rraga_Palmero) became a hero as he restored the [Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Bourbon) dynasty of Spain to the throne during his stint as Lieutenant-General (Three Star General) after the Bourbons have been deposed by revolutionaries. He eventually became Prime Minister of the Spanish Empire and was awarded membership in the [Order of the Golden Fleece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_the_Golden_Fleece), which is considered the most exclusive and prestigious order of chivalry in the world.[^cite_note-Manila,_My_Manila-346] In the aftermath of Chilean soldiers' participation in the [Andres Novales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andres_Novales) uprising against Spain, the Irish-Chilean founder of Chile, [Bernardo O'Higgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_O'Higgins), caught wind of anti-Spanish sentiment among Filipinos and planned to send a fleet to liberate the Philippines from Spain, under the command of Scottish-Chilean admiral, Lord Thomas Cochrane. The fleet would have been sent to the Philippines had it not been for [Bernardo O'Higgins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernardo_O'Higgins)' untimely exile.[^cite_note-347]

### Philippine Revolution

Main article: [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution)



[Andrés Bonifacio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Bonifacio), father of the Philippine Revolution.

Revolutionary sentiments arose in 1872 after three Filipino priests, [Mariano Gomez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Gomez_\(priest\)), [José Burgos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Burgos), and [Jacinto Zamora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacinto_Zamora), known as [Gomburza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gomburza), were accused of sedition by colonial authorities and executed by [garotte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garotte). This would inspire the [Propaganda Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_Movement) in Spain, organized by [Marcelo H. del Pilar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_H._del_Pilar), [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal), [Graciano López Jaena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graciano_L%C3%B3pez_Jaena), and [Mariano Ponce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Ponce), that clamored for adequate representation to the [Spanish Cortes](519703.md) and later for independence. [José Rizal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Rizal), the most celebrated intellectual and radical ilustrado of the era, wrote the novels "[Noli Me Tángere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noli_Me_T%C3%A1ngere_\(novel\))" and "[El filibusterismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_filibusterismo)", which greatly inspired the movement for independence.[^cite_note-pinas-348] The [Katipunan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katipunan), a [secret society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secret_society) whose primary purpose was that of overthrowing Spanish rule in the Philippines, was founded by [Andrés Bonifacio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Bonifacio) who became its *Supremo* (leader).

In the 1860s to 1890s, in the urban areas of the Philippines, especially at Manila, according to burial statistics, as much as 3.3% of the population were pure European Spaniards and the pure Chinese were as high as 9.9%. The Spanish-Filipino and Chinese-Filipino Mestizo populations also fluctuated. Eventually, everybody belonging to these non-native categories diminished because they were assimilated into and chose to self-identify as pure Filipinos[^cite_note-349] since during the Philippine Revolution, the term "Filipino" included anybody born in the Philippines coming from any race.[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] That would explain the abrupt drop of otherwise high Chinese, Spanish, and mestizo percentages across the country by the time of the first American census in 1903.[^cite_note-352]

The [Philippine Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolution) began in 1896. Rizal was wrongly implicated in the outbreak of the revolution and executed for [treason](31292.md) in 1896. The Katipunan in [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite) split into two groups, [Magdiwang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magdiwang_\(Katipunan_faction\)), led by [Mariano Álvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_%C3%81lvarez) (a relative of Bonifacio's by marriage), and [Magdalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magdalo_\(Katipunan_faction\)), led by [Baldomero Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldomero_Aguinaldo) cousin of [Emilio Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Aguinaldo). Tension between the factions led to the [Tejeros Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tejeros_Convention) in 1897, at which an election chose Emilio Aguinaldo as president over Bonifacio and Trias. Subsequent leadership conflicts with Bonifacio culminated in his execution by Aguinaldo's soldiers. Aguinaldo agreed to a truce with the [Pact of Biak-na-Bato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pact_of_Biak-na-Bato) and Aguinaldo and his fellow revolutionaries were [exiled to Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_Junta). Not all the revolutionary generals complied with the agreement. One, General [Francisco Macabulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Macabulos), established a [Central Executive Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Executive_Committee_\(Philippines\)) to serve as the [interim government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_government) until a more suitable one was created. Armed conflicts resumed, this time coming from almost every province in Spanish-governed Philippines.



Revolutionaries gather during the [Malolos Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malolos_Congress_\(1898\)) of the [Revolutionary Government of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Government_of_the_Philippines).

In 1898, as conflicts continued in the Philippines, the [USS *Maine*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_\(ACR-1\)), having been sent to Cuba because of U.S. concerns for the safety of its citizens during an ongoing [Cuban revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_War_of_Independence), exploded and sank in [Havana](49719.md) harbor. This event precipitated the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War).[^cite_note-353] After [Commodore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodore_\(rank\)) [George Dewey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Dewey) defeated the Spanish squadron at Manila, a [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) squadron arrived in Manila and engaged in maneuvers which Dewey, seeing this as obstruction of his blockade, offered war—after which the Germans backed down.[^cite_note-354] The German Emperor expected an American defeat, with Spain left in a sufficiently weak position for the revolutionaries to capture Manila—leaving the Philippines ripe for German picking.[^cite_note-355]

The U.S. invited Aguinaldo to return to the Philippines in the hope he would rally Filipinos against the Spanish colonial government. Aguinaldo arrived on May 19, 1898, via transport provided by Dewey. On June 12, 1898, Aguinaldo [declared the independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Declaration_of_Independence) of the Philippines in [Kawit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawit), [Cavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavite). Aguinaldo proclaimed a [Revolutionary Government of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Government_of_the_Philippines) on June 23. By the time U.S. land forces arrived, the Filipinos had taken control of the entire island of Luzon except for the Spanish capitol in the walled city of [Intramuros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuros). In the [Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1898\)), on August 13, 1898, the United States captured the city from the Spanish. This battle marked an end of Filipino-American collaboration, as Filipino forces were prevented from entering the captured city of Manila, an action deeply resented by the Filipinos.[^cite_note-lac126-356]

## The First Philippine Republic (1899–1901)



[Emilio Aguinaldo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilio_Aguinaldo), President of the [First Philippine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Philippine_Republic)

On January 23, 1899, the [First Philippine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Philippine_Republic) was proclaimed under Asia's first democratic constitution, with Aguinaldo as its president.[^cite_note-pinas-348] Under Aguinaldo, The [Philippine Revolutionary Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolutionary_Army) was also renowned to be racially tolerant and progressive as it had a multi-ethnic composition that included various other races and nationalities asides from the native Filipino,[^cite_note-filipinoterm-256] being its officers. [Juan Cailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Cailles) an Indian and French Mestizo served as a [Major General](36301471.md),[^cite_note-Foreman-357] the Chinese Filipino [José Ignacio Paua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ignacio_Paua) was a [Brigadier General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigadier_General),[^cite_note-358] and [Vicente Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Catalan) who was appointed supreme Admiral of the Philippine Revolutionary Navy was a Cuban of Criollo descent.[^cite_note-359] There were even Japanese, French and Italian soldiers in the Revolution and Republic, such as the Japanese officer Captain Chizuno Iwamoto, French soldier Estaquio Castellor, and Italian revolutionary Captain [Camillo Ricchiardi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camillo_Ricchiardi). And, even among the defeated Spanish Army and American invaders, [there are those who defected to the side of the Philippine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolutionary_Army#Notable_officers_and_servicemen_and_their_ethnic_background). The most famous of which was African-American Captain [David Fagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Fagen) who joined the Filipinos due to his disgust of American racism against both African-Americans and Filipinos. Various nations, mostly Latin American, also influenced the new Republic, the Sun in the Philippine flag was taken from the [Sun of May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_of_May) of [Peru](170691.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), and [Uruguay](31843.md) which symbolized Inti who was the Incan Sun God, while the stars in the flag were inspired by the stars in the flags of the nations of [Texas](29810.md), [Cuba](5042481.md), and [Puerto Rico](23041.md).[^cite_note-360] The Constitution of the First Philippine Republic was also influenced by the Constitutions of [Cuba](5042481.md), [Belgium](3343.md), [Mexico](3966054.md), [Brazil](3383.md), [Nicaragua](21362.md), [Costa Rica](5551.md), and [Guatemala](17238567.md), in addition to using the [French Constitution of 1793](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Constitution_of_1793).[^cite_note-361]



An early flag of the Filipino revolutionaries.

Despite the establishment of the First Philippine Republic, Spain and the United States had sent commissioners to Paris to draw up the terms of the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1898\)) to end the Spanish–American War. The Filipino representative, [Felipe Agoncillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Agoncillo), had been excluded from sessions as Aguinaldo's government was not recognized by the family of nations.[^cite_note-lac126-356] Although there was substantial domestic opposition, the United States decided to annex the Philippines. Despite the fact that the first Philippine Republic was patterned after the French and American Revolutions, plus the Latin-American Republics; the Americans and French themselves sought to crush the revolution in the Philippines.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalaw1927199–200-362] In addition to [Guam](11974.md) and Puerto Rico, Spain was forced in the negotiations to [cede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cession) the Philippines to the U.S. in exchange for US\$20,000,000.00.[^cite_note-uslc-14-363] U.S. President McKinley justified the annexation of the Philippines by saying that it was "a gift from the gods" and that since "they were unfit for self-government, ... there was nothing left for us to do but to take them all, and to educate the Filipinos, and uplift and civilize and Christianize them",[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] even though the Philippines had already been Christianized by the Spanish over the course of several centuries. The First Philippine Republic resisted the U.S. occupation, resulting in the [Philippine–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War) (1899–1913).

## American rule (1898–1946)

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1898–1946)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1898%E2%80%931946\))



1898 political cartoon showing U.S. President [McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley) with a native child. Here, returning the Philippines to Spain is compared to throwing the child off a cliff.

Filipinos initially saw their relationship with the United States as that of two nations joined in a common struggle against Spain.[^cite_note-366] However, the United States later distanced itself from the interests of the Filipino insurgents. Emilio Aguinaldo was unhappy that the United States would not commit to paper a statement of support for [Philippine independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-uslc-13-367] The islands were ceded by Spain to the United States alongside [Puerto Rico](23041.md) and [Guam](11974.md) as a result of the latter's victory in the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War).[^cite_note-368] A compensation of US\$20 million was paid to Spain according to the terms of the [1898 Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1898\)).[^cite_note-369] Relations deteriorated and tensions heightened as it became clear that the Americans were in the islands to stay.[^cite_note-uslc-13-367]

### Philippine–American War

Main article: [Philippine–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War)



Filipino casualties on the first day of war

Hostilities broke out on February 4, 1899, after two American privates killed three Filipino soldiers as American forces launched a major attack in [San Juan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Juan,_Metro_Manila), a [Manila](184334.md) suburb.[^cite_note-uslc-15-370] This began the [Philippine–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine%E2%80%93American_War), which cost far more money and took far more lives than the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War).[^cite_note-pinas-348] Some 126,000 American soldiers would be committed to the conflict; 4,234 Americans died,[^cite_note-uslc-15-370] as did 12,000–20,000 [Philippine Republican Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Revolutionary_Army) soldiers who were part of a nationwide [guerrilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla) movement of at least 80,000 to 100,000 soldiers.[^cite_note-deady2005p55-371]

The general population, caught between Americans and rebels, suffered significantly. At least 200,000 Filipino civilians died as an indirect result of the war mostly as a result of the [cholera](7591.md) epidemic at the war's end that took between 150,000 and 200,000 lives.[^cite_note-372] Atrocities were committed by both sides.[^cite_note-uslc-15-370]



American troops guarding the bridge over the River Pasig on the afternoon of the surrender. From *Harper's Pictorial History of the War with Spain*, Vol. II, published by Harper and Brothers in 1899.
