The poorly equipped Filipino troops were easily overpowered by American troops in open combat, but they were formidable opponents in guerrilla warfare.[^cite_note-uslc-15-370] [Malolos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malolos_City), the revolutionary capital, was captured on March 31, 1899. Aguinaldo and his government escaped, however, establishing a new capital at [San Isidro, Nueva Ecija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Isidro,_Nueva_Ecija). On June 5, 1899, [Antonio Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Luna), Aguinaldo's most capable military commander, was killed by Aguinaldo's guards in an apparent assassination while visiting [Cabanatuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabanatuan), [Nueva Ecija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nueva_Ecija) to meet with Aguinaldo.[^cite_note-373] With his best commander dead and his troops suffering continued defeats as American forces pushed into northern [Luzon](69375.md), Aguinaldo dissolved the regular army on November 13 and ordered the establishment of decentralized guerrilla commands in each of several military zones.[^cite_note-374] Another key general, [Gregorio del Pilar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorio_del_Pilar), was killed on December 2, 1899, in the [Battle of Tirad Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tirad_Pass)—a [rear guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rear_guard) action to delay the Americans while Aguinaldo made good his escape through the mountains.



President Emilio Aguinaldo boarding the [USS Vicksburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Vicksburg_\(PG-11\)) after his capture by American forces.

Aguinaldo was captured at [Palanan, Isabela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palanan,_Isabela) on March 23, 1901, and was brought to Manila. Convinced of the futility of further resistance, he swore allegiance to the United States and issued a proclamation calling on his compatriots to lay down their arms, officially bringing an end to the war.[^cite_note-uslc-15-370] However, sporadic insurgent resistance continued in various parts of the Philippines, especially in the Muslim south, until 1913.[^cite_note-375]

In 1900, President Roosevelt sent [William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft) as head of what would come to be know as the [Taft Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taft_Commission) to the Philippines with a mandate to legislate laws and re-engineer the political system. Roosevelt also sent his daughter, Alice under Taft's watchful eye to visit the islands[^cite_note-376] On July 1, 1901, Taft, the head of the commission, was inaugurated the Civil Governor, with limited executive powers.[^cite_note-377] The authority of the Military Governor was continued in those areas where the insurrection persisted.[^cite_note-378] The Taft Commission passed laws to set up the fundamentals of the new government, including a judicial system, civil service, and local government. A [Philippine Constabulary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Constabulary) was organized to deal with the remnants of the insurgent movement and gradually assume the responsibilities of the [United States Army](32087.md).[^cite_note-379]

### The Tagalog, Negros, and Zamboanga Republics

Brigadier General [James F. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_F._Smith) arrived at Bacolod on March 4, 1899, as the [Military Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Governor) of the Sub-district of Negros, after receiving an invitation from [Aniceto Lacson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniceto_Lacson), president of the breakaway [Cantonal Republic of Negros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Negros).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELinn200075–76-380] The Negros Republic became a Pro-American protectorate of the United States.[^cite_note-381] Another insurgent republic was briefly formed during American administration: the [Tagalog Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_Republic) in Luzon, under [Macario Sakay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macario_Sakay).[^cite_note-antonio_abad-382] In the island of Mindanao the [Chavacano-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavacano_Language) [Republic of Zamboanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Zamboanga) was proclaimed.[^cite_note-383] That government was formed by [Vicente Álvarez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_%C3%81lvarez_\(general\)) with the support of [Jamalul Kiram II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamalul_Kiram_II), the then [Sultan of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu), and included Latin-American (Peruvian, [Uruguayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Fermin_de_San_Martin) and [Argentinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn#Legacy))[^cite_note-384] enslaved soldiers who had revolted against the Spanish colonial government and included Muslims, Lumads, and Christians in his anti-Spanish army.[^cite_note-385]

### Insular Government (1901–1935)

Main article: [Insular Government of the Philippine Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Government_of_the_Philippine_Islands)



[William Howard Taft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Howard_Taft) addressing the audience at the [Philippine Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Assembly).



Representatives from the Philippine Independence Mission left to right: [Isauro Gabaldón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isauro_Gabald%C3%B3n), [Sergio Osmeña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Osme%C3%B1a), [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon), [Claro M. Recto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claro_M._Recto), [Pedro Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Guevara), Jorge Bocobo

The [Philippine Organic Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Organic_Act_\(1902\)) was the basic law for the [Insular Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Government), so called because civil administration was under the authority of the U.S. [Bureau of Insular Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Insular_Affairs). This government saw its mission as one of tutelage, preparing the Philippines for eventual independence.[^cite_note-uslc-16-386] On July 4, 1902, the office of military governor was abolished and full executive power passed from [Adna Chaffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adna_Chaffee), the last military governor, to Taft, who became the first U.S. [Governor-General of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-387] United States policies towards the Philippines shifted with changing administrations.[^cite_note-pinas-348] During the early years of territorial administration, the Americans were reluctant to delegate authority to the Filipinos, but an elected [Philippine Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Assembly) was inaugurated in 1907, as the lower house of a [bicameral legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral_legislature), with the appointive Philippine Commission becoming the upper house.

Philippines was a major target for the progressive reformers. A 1907 report to Secretary of War Taft provided a summary of what the American civil administration had achieved. It included, in addition to the rapid building of a public school system based on English teaching, and boasted about such modernizing achievements as:

> steel and concrete wharves at the newly renovated [Port of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Manila); dredging the [River Pasig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Pasig); streamlining of the Insular Government; accurate, intelligible accounting; the construction of a telegraph and cable communications network; the establishment of a postal savings bank; large-scale road-and bridge-building; impartial and incorrupt policing; well-financed civil engineering; the conservation of old Spanish architecture; large public parks; a bidding process for the right to build railways; Corporation law; and a coastal and geological survey.[^cite_note-388]

In 1903 the American reformers in the Philippines passed two major land acts designed to turn landless peasants into owners of their farms. By 1905 the law was clearly a failure. Reformers such as Taft believed landownership would turn unruly agrarians into loyal subjects. The social structure in the rural Philippines was highly traditional and highly unequal. Drastic changes in landownership posed a major challenge to local elites, who would not accept it, nor would their peasant clients. The American reformers blamed peasant resistance to landownership for the law's failure and argued that large plantations and sharecropping was the Philippines' best path to development.[^cite_note-389]



A [tranvía](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tranv%C3%ADa) in Manila during the American colonial period.

Elite Filipina women played a major role in the reform movement, especially on health issues. They specialized on such urgent needs as infant care and maternal and child health, the distribution of pure milk and teaching new mothers about children's health. The most prominent organizations were the La Protección de la Infancia, and the National Federation of Women's Clubs.[^cite_note-390]

When Democrat [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) became U.S. president in 1913, new policies were launched designed to gradually lead to Philippine independence. In 1902 U.S. law established Filipinos citizenship in the Philippine Islands; unlike Hawaii in 1898 and Puerto Rico in 1918, they did not become citizens of the United States. The [Jones Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jones_Act_\(Philippines\)) of 1916 became the new basic law, promising eventual independence. It provided for the election of both houses of the legislature.



Manila, Philippines, c. 1900s

In socio-economic terms, the Philippines made solid progress in this period. Foreign trade had amounted to 62 million pesos in 1895, 13% of which was with the United States. By 1920, it had increased to 601 million pesos, 66% of which was with the United States.[^cite_note-391] A health care system was established which, by 1930, reduced the [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate) from all causes, including various [tropical diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_disease), to a level similar to that of the United States itself. The practices of [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery), [piracy](50715.md) and [headhunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headhunting) were suppressed but not entirely extinguished. A new educational system was established with English as the medium of instruction, eventually becoming a *lingua franca* of the Islands. The 1920s saw alternating periods of cooperation and confrontation with American governors-general, depending on how intent the incumbent was on exercising his powers vis-à-vis the Philippine legislature. Members of the elected legislature lobbied for immediate and complete independence from the United States. Several independence missions were sent to Washington, D.C. A civil service was formed and was gradually taken over by Filipinos, who had effectively gained control by 1918.



[El Hogar Building.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Hogar_Filipino_Building) With Manila's Hispanic-Austronesian-Sinic roots, [Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) built a plan that takes advantage of its cityscape, possessing the [Bay of Naples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Naples), the winding river of [Paris](22989.md), and the canals of [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venice). With his [City Beautiful movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Beautiful_movement) style of Urban planning.

Philippine politics during the American territorial era was dominated by the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party), which was founded in 1907. Although the party's platform called for "immediate independence", their policy toward the Americans was highly accommodating.[^cite_note-uslc-17-392] Within the political establishment, the call for independence was spearheaded by [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon), who served continuously as [Senate president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_president) from 1916 until 1935.

World War I gave the Philippines the opportunity to pledge assistance to the US war effort. This took the form of an offer to supply a division of troops, as well as providing funding for the construction of two warships. A locally recruited national guard was created and significant numbers of Filipinos volunteered for service in the US Navy and army.[^cite_note-393]

[Daniel Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Burnham) built an architectural plan for Manila which would have transformed it into a modern city.[^cite_note-394]

[Frank Murphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Murphy) was the last [Governor-General of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor-General_of_the_Philippines) (1933–35), and the first U.S. [High Commissioner of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Commissioner_of_the_Philippines) (1935–36). The change in form was more than symbolic: it was intended as a manifestation of the transition to independence.

### Commonwealth

Main article: [Commonwealth of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_the_Philippines)



Philippine President [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon)

The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) in the early thirties hastened the progress of the Philippines towards independence. In the United States it was mainly the sugar industry and labor unions that had a stake in loosening the U.S. ties to the Philippines since they could not compete with the Philippine cheap sugar (and other commodities) which could freely enter the U.S. market. Therefore, they agitated in favor of granting independence to the Philippines so that its cheap products and labor could be shut out of the United States.[^cite_note-395]



Commonwealth President [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon) with United States President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) in Washington, D.C.

In 1933, the [United States Congress](31756.md) passed the [Hare–Hawes–Cutting Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hare%E2%80%93Hawes%E2%80%93Cutting_Act) as a Philippine Independence Act over President [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover)'s veto.[^cite_note-396] Though the bill had been drafted with the aid of a commission from the Philippines, it was opposed by Philippine Senate President [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon), partially because of provisions leaving the United States in control of naval bases. Under his influence, the Philippine legislature rejected the bill.[^cite_note-uslc-20-397] The following year, a revised act known as the [Tydings–McDuffie Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tydings%E2%80%93McDuffie_Act) was finally passed. The act provided for the establishment of the [Commonwealth of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_the_Philippines) with transition to full independence after a ten-year period. The commonwealth would have its own constitution and be self-governing, though foreign policy would be the responsibility of the United States, and certain legislation required approval of the United States president.[^cite_note-uslc-20-397] The Act stipulated that the date of independence would be on July 4 following the tenth anniversary of the establishment of the Commonwealth.



[Philippine First Lady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_First_Lady) [Aurora Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora_Quezon)

A Constitutional Convention was convened in Manila on July 30, 1934. On February 8, 1935, the 1935 Constitution of the Republic of the Philippines was approved by the convention by a vote of 177 to 1. The constitution was approved by President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) on March 23, 1935, and ratified by popular vote on May 14, 1935.[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399]

On September 17, 1935,[^cite_note-presidency.ucsb.edu-400] [presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1935_Philippine_presidential_election) were held. Candidates included former president Emilio Aguinaldo, the *[Iglesia Filipina Independiente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Independent_Church)* leader [Gregorio Aglipay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorio_Aglipay), and others. [Manuel L. Quezon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_L._Quezon) and [Sergio Osmeña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Osme%C3%B1a) of the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party) were proclaimed the winners, winning the seats of president and vice-president, respectively.[^cite_note-US-influence-401]

The Commonwealth Government was inaugurated on the morning of November 15, 1935, in ceremonies held on the steps of the [Legislative Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Legislative_Building_\(Manila\)) in Manila. The event was attended by a crowd of around 300,000 people.[^cite_note-presidency.ucsb.edu-400] Under the Tydings–McDuffie Act this meant that the date of full independence for the Philippines was set for July 4, 1946, a timetable which was followed after the passage of almost eleven very eventful years.



Legislative Building of the commonwealth of the Philippines

The new government embarked on ambitious nation-building policies in preparation for economic and political independence.[^cite_note-US-influence2-402] These included national defense (such as the [National Defense Act of 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Defense_Act_of_1935), which organized a [conscription](5735.md) for service in the country), greater control over the [economy](23445.md), the perfection of democratic institutions, reforms in education, improvement of transport, the promotion of local capital, industrialization, and the colonization of [Mindanao](20546.md).

However, uncertainties, especially in the diplomatic and military situation in [Southeast Asia](28741.md), in the level of U.S. commitment to the future [Republic of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_the_Philippines), and in the economy due to the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), proved to be major problems. The situation was further complicated by the presence of agrarian unrest, and of power struggles between Osmeña and Quezon,[^cite_note-US-influence2-402] especially after Quezon was permitted to be re-elected after one six-year term.

A proper evaluation of the policies' effectiveness or failure is difficult due to Japanese invasion and [occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines) during World War II.

### World War II and Japanese occupation

Main articles: [Japanese occupation of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines), [Second Philippine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Philippine_Republic), and [Home front during World War II § The Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_front_during_World_War_II#The_Philippines)

#### Military



Japanese bombers over Corregidor

[Japan](15573.md) launched a surprise [attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Philippines_\(1942\)) on the [Clark Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Air_Base) in [Pampanga](255602.md) on the morning of December 8, 1941, just ten hours after the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor). Aerial bombardment was followed by landings of ground troops on Luzon. The defending Philippine and United States troops were under the command of General [Douglas MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_MacArthur). Under the pressure of superior numbers, the defending forces withdrew to the [Bataan Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan_Peninsula) and to the island of [Corregidor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corregidor) at the entrance to Manila Bay.

On January 2, 1942, General MacArthur declared the capital city, Manila, an [open city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_city) to prevent its destruction.[^cite_note-403] The Philippine defense continued until the final surrender of United States-Philippine forces on the [Bataan Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan_Peninsula) in April 1942 and on Corregidor in May of the same year. Most of the 80,000 prisoners of war captured by the Japanese at Bataan were forced to undertake the infamous [Bataan Death March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan_Death_March) to a prison camp 105 kilometers to the north. About 10,000 Filipinos and 1,200 Americans died before reaching their destination.[^cite_note-404] President Quezon and Osmeña had accompanied the troops to Corregidor and later left for the United States, where they set up a government in exile.[^cite_note-405] MacArthur was ordered to Australia, where he started to plan for a return to the Philippines.



Exiled Manuel L. Quezon (sitting second to the right) in Washington, D.C., with Representatives of 26 United Nations at Flag day ceremonies in the [White House](33057.md) to reaffirm their pact.&#x20;

The Japanese military authorities immediately began organizing a new government structure in the Philippines and established the [Philippine Executive Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Executive_Commission). They initially organized a [Council of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Council_of_State), through which they directed civil affairs until October 1943, when Japan declared the Philippines an independent republic at Gozen Kaigi since U.S. government had promised independence of the Philippines in 1935. The Japanese-sponsored republic headed by President [José P. Laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_P._Laurel) proved to be unpopular.[^cite_note-uslc-21-406]

From mid-1942 through mid-1944, Japanese occupation of the Philippines was opposed by large-scale [underground and guerrilla activity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines#Resistance).[^cite_note-McAAE-407][^cite_note-408] The [Philippine Army](6032559.md), as well as remnants of the [U.S. Army Forces Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Army_Forces_Far_East),[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410] continued to fight the Japanese in a guerrilla war and was considered an auxiliary unit of the United States Army.[^cite_note-411] Supplies and encouragement were provided by U.S. Navy submarines and a few parachute drops.[^cite_note-412] Their effectiveness was such that by the end of the war, Japan controlled only twelve of the forty-eight [provinces](165100.md).[^cite_note-uslc-21-406] One element of resistance in the Central Luzon area was furnished by the [Hukbalahap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukbalahap), which armed some 30,000 people and extended their control over much of Luzon.[^cite_note-uslc-21-406] While remaining loyal to the United States, many Filipinos hoped and believed that liberation from the Japanese would bring them freedom and their already-promised independence.[^cite_note-Dispositions-413]



As many as 10,000 American and Filipino soldiers died in the [Bataan Death March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bataan_Death_March)

The Philippines was the bloodiest theater of the war for the invading empire, with at least 498,600 Japanese troops killed in fighting the combined Filipino resistance and American soldiers, a larger number of casualties compared to the second-placed theater, the entirety of China, which caused the Japanese about 455,700 casualties.[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-Dispositions-413] The occupation of the Philippines by Japan ended at the war's conclusion. At the eve of the liberation of the Philippines, the Allied forces and the Japanese Empire waged the largest naval battle in history, by gross tonnage in the [Battle of Leyte Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Leyte_Gulf).[^cite_note-417] The American army had been fighting the [Philippines Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_Campaign_\(1944%E2%80%9345\)) since October 1944, when MacArthur's [Sixth United States Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sixth_United_States_Army) [landed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Leyte) on [Leyte](194750.md). Landings in other parts of the country had followed, and the Allies, with the Philippine Commonwealth troops, pushed toward Manila. However, fighting continued until Japan's formal surrender on September 2, 1945. Approximately 10,000 U.S. soldiers were missing in action in the Philippines when the war ended, more than in any other country in the Pacific or European Theaters. The Philippines suffered great loss of life and tremendous physical destruction, especially during the [Battle of Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Manila_\(1945\)). An estimated 1 million Filipinos had been killed, a large portion during the final months of the war, and Manila had been extensively damaged.[^cite_note-uslc-21-406]

#### Home front



[Leyte](194750.md) Landing of General [Douglas MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_MacArthur) to liberate the Philippines from the Empire of Japan

As in most occupied countries, crime, looting, corruption, and black markets were endemic. Japan in 1943 proposed independence on new terms, and some collaborators went along with the plan, but Japan was clearly losing the war and nothing became of it.[^cite_note-418]

With a view of building up the economic base of the [Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_East_Asia_Co-Prosperity_Sphere), the Japanese Army envisioned using the islands as a source of agricultural products needed by its industry. For example, the Japanese had a surplus of sugar from Taiwan but a severe shortage of cotton, so they tried to grow cotton on sugar lands with disastrous results. They lacked the seeds, [pesticides](48340.md), and technical skills to grow cotton. Jobless farm workers flocked to the cities, where there was minimal relief and few jobs. The Japanese Army also tried using cane sugar for fuel, [castor beans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castor_bean) and [copra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copra) for oil, [derris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derris) for [quinine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinine), cotton for uniforms, and [abaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaca) (hemp) for rope. The plans were very difficult to implement in the face of limited skills, collapsed international markets, bad weather, and transportation shortages. The program was a failure that gave very little help to Japanese industry, and diverted resources needed for food production.

Living conditions were bad throughout the Philippines during the war. Transportation between the islands was difficult because of lack of fuel. Food was in very short supply, due to inflation.[^cite_note-419]

## The Third Republic (1946–1965)

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1946–1965)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1946%E2%80%931965\))



The Flag of the United States of America is lowered while the Flag of the Philippines is raised during the Independence Day ceremonies on July 4, 1946

The return of the Americans in spring 1945 was welcomed by nearly all the Filipinos, in sharp contrast to the situation in nearby Dutch East Indies. The collaborationist "Philippine Republic" set up by the Japanese under [Jose P. Laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_P._Laurel), was highly unpopular, and the extreme destructiveness of the Japanese Army in Manila in its last days solidified Japan's image as a permanent target of hate. The pre-war Commonwealth system was reestablished under [Sergio Osmeña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Osme%C3%B1a), who became president in exile after President Quezon died in 1944. Osmeña was little-known and his [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party) was no longer such a dominant force. Osmeña supporters challenged the legitimacy of Manuel Roxas who had served as secretary to Laurel. MacArthur testified to Roxas' patriotism and the collaborationist issue disappeared after Roxas was elected in 1946 on a platform calling for closer ties with the United States; adherence to the new United Nations; national reconstruction; relief for the masses; social justice for the working class; the maintenance of peace and order; the preservation of individual rights and liberties of the citizenry; and honesty and efficiency of government.[^cite_note-420] The United States Congress passed a series of programs to help rehabilitation, including \$2 billion over five years for war damages and rehabilitation, and a new tariff law that provided for a 20-year transition from free trade to a low tariff with the United States. Washington also demanded that Americans would have equal rights with Filipinos in business activities, a special treatment that was resented. In 1947 the United States secured an agreement that it would keep its major military and naval bases. On the whole the transition to independence, achieved in 1946, was mostly peaceful and highly successful, despite the extreme difficulties caused by massive war damages.[^cite_note-421] The special relationship with the United States remained the dominant feature until sharp criticism arose in the 1960s.[^cite_note-422]

### Administration of Manuel Roxas (1946–1948)



Manuel Roxas inaugurated as president in 1946

Elections were held in April 1946, with [Manuel Roxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Roxas) becoming the first president of the independent Republic of the Philippines. The United States [ceded its sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Manila_\(1946\)) over the Philippines on July 4, 1946, as scheduled. Ending the 381 years of colonial rule in the country that had lasted from April 27, 1565, since the Spanish settlement.[^cite_note-pinas-348][^cite_note-423] However, the [Philippine economy](23445.md) remained highly dependent on [United States markets](32022.md)—more dependent, according to United States high commissioner [Paul McNutt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_McNutt), than any single U.S. state was dependent on the rest of the country.[^cite_note-uslc-23-424] The [Philippine Trade Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Trade_Act), passed as a precondition for receiving war rehabilitation grants from the United States,[^cite_note-beterano-425] exacerbated the dependency with provisions further tying the economies of the two countries. A military assistance pact was signed in 1947 granting the United States a 99-year lease on designated [military bases](843566.md) in the country.

During Roxas' term of office administration of the [Turtle Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle_Islands,_Tawi-Tawi) and [Mangsee Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangsee_Islands) was transferred by the [United Kingdom](31717.md) to the [Republic of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_the_Philippines). By an international treaty concluded in 1930 between the [United States](3434750.md) (in respect of its then overseas territory, the [Philippine Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Archipelago)) and the [United Kingdom](31717.md) (in respect of its then protectorate, the [State of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo)) the two powers agreed the international boundaries between those respective territories.[^cite_note-426] In that treaty the United Kingdom also accepted that the Turtle Islands as well as the Mangsee Islands were part of the Philippines Archipelago and therefore under US sovereignty. However, by a supplemental international treaty concluded at the same time, the two powers agreed that those islands, although part of the Philippines Archipelago, would remain under the administration of the [State of North Borneo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo)'s [British North Borneo Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_Borneo_Company).[^cite_note-427] The supplemental treaty provided that the British North Borneo Company would continue to administer those islands unless and until the United States government gave notice to the United Kingdom calling for administration of the islands to be transferred to the U.S. The U.S. never gave such a notice. On July 4, 1946, the [Republic of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_the_Philippines) was born. It became the successor to the U.S. under the treaties of 1930. On July 15, 1946, the United Kingdom annexed the State of North Borneo and, in the view of the United Kingdom, became the sovereign power with respect to what had been the State of North Borneo.[^cite_note-428] On September 19, 1946, the Republic of the Philippines notified the United Kingdom that it wished to take over the administration of the [Turtle Islands, Tawi-Tawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turtle_Islands,_Tawi-Tawi) and the Mangesse Islands. Pursuant to a supplemental international agreement, the transfer of administration became effective on October 16, 1947.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430]

Roxas did not stay long in office, dying of a heart attack soon after a speech at Clark Air Base on April 15, 1948. He was succeeded by his vice president [Elpidio Quirino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elpidio_Quirino).[^cite_note-431]

### Administration of Elpidio Quirino (1948–1953)



President Quirino (in the center-left) and family in Malacañang Palace.

The Roxas administration granted general amnesty to those who had collaborated with the Japanese in World War II, except for those who had committed violent crimes. Roxas died suddenly of a heart attack in April 1948, and the vice president, [Elpidio Quirino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elpidio_Quirino), was elevated to the presidency. He ran for president in his own right in 1949, defeating [José P. Laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_P._Laurel) and winning a four-year term.

World War II had left the Philippines demoralized and severely damaged. The task of reconstruction was complicated by the activities of the Communist-supported [Hukbalahap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukbalahap) guerrillas (known as "Huks"), who had evolved into a violent resistance force against the new Philippine government. Government policy towards the Huks alternated between gestures of negotiation and harsh suppression. Secretary of Defense [Ramon Magsaysay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay) initiated a campaign to defeat the insurgents militarily and at the same time win popular support for the government. The Huk movement had waned in the early 1950s, finally ending with the unconditional surrender of Huk leader [Luis Taruc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Taruc) in May 1954.

Enhancing President [Manuel Roxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Roxas)' policy of social justice to alleviate the lot of the common mass, President Quirino, almost immediately after assuming office, started a series of steps calculated to effectively ameliorate the economic condition of the people.[^cite_note-Molina,_Antonio_19612-432] After periodic surprise visits to the slums of [Manila](184334.md) and other backward regions of the country, President Quirino officially made public a seven-point program for social security, to wit:[^cite_note-Molina,_Antonio_19612-432] unemployment insurance, old-age insurance, accident and permanent disability insurance, health insurance, maternity insurance, state relief, and labor opportunity.

President Quirino also created the Social Security Commission, making Social Welfare Commissioner Asuncion Perez chairman of the same.[^cite_note-Molina,_Antonio_19612-432] This was followed by the creation of the President's Action Committee on Social Amelioration, charged with extending aid, loans, and relief to the less fortunate citizens. Both the policy and its implementation were hailed by the people as harbingers of great benefits.[^cite_note-Molina,_Antonio_19612-432]

### Administration of Ramon Magsaysay (1953–1957)



President [Magsaysay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay) (right) in 1957

As President, he was a close friend and supporter of the United States and a vocal spokesman against communism during the [Cold War](325329.md). He led the foundation of the [Southeast Asia Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization), also known as the Manila Pact of 1954, that aimed to defeat communist-Marxist movements in Southeast Asia, South Asia and the Southwestern Pacific.

During his term, he made [Malacañang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1an_Palace) literally a "house of the people", opening its gates to the public. One example of his integrity followed a demonstration flight aboard a new plane belonging to the [Philippine Air Force](27918720.md) (PAF): President Magsaysay asked what the operating costs per hour were for that type of aircraft, then wrote a personal check to the PAF, covering the cost of his flight. He restored the people's trust in the military and in the government.

Magsaysay's administration was considered one of the cleanest and most corruption-free in modern Philippine history; his rule is often cited as the Philippines' "Golden Years". Trade and industry flourished, the [Philippine military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_military) was at its prime, and the country gained international recognition in sports, culture, and foreign affairs. The Philippines placed second on a ranking of Asia's clean and well-governed countries.

Supported by the United States, Magsaysay was elected president in 1953 on a [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) platform. He promised sweeping economic reform, and made progress in [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform_in_the_Philippines) by promoting the resettlement of poor people in the Catholic north into traditionally Muslim areas. Though this relieved population pressure in the north, it heightened religious hostilities.[^cite_note-uslc-26-433] Remnants of the communist [Hukbalahap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukbalahap)[^cite_note-434] were defeated by Magsaysay.[^cite_note-Molina,_Antonio_1961-435][^cite_note-436] He was extremely popular with the common people, and his death in an [airplane crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_Cebu_Douglas_C-47_crash) in March 1957 dealt a serious blow to national morale.[^cite_note-437] At this time, the Philippines joined the United Nations in defending South Korea from North Korean invasions. The Philippines was the first country in Southeast Asia to recognize South Korean independence and was the first to send military units to fight on [South Korea](27019.md)'s behalf.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439]

### Administration of Carlos P. Garcia (1957–1961)



Garcia inaugurated as president in 1957

[Carlos P. Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_P._Garcia) succeeded to the presidency after Magsaysay's death, and was elected to a four-year term in the election of November that same year. His administration emphasized the nationalist theme of "Filipino first", arguing that the Filipino people should be given the chances to improve the country's economy.[^cite_note-cgarcia-440]

Garcia successfully negotiated for the United States' relinquishment of large military land reservations. However, his administration lost popularity on issues of government corruption as his term advanced.[^cite_note-441]

### Administration of Diosdado Macapagal (1961–1965)



Diosdado Macapagal departing for Malacañang

In the presidential elections held on November 14, 1961, Vice President [Diosdado Macapagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Macapagal) defeated re-electionist President [Carlos P. Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_P._Garcia) and [Emmanuel Pelaez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Pelaez) as a vice president. President [Macapagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macapagal) changed the [independence day of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Philippines\)) from July 4 to June 12.

The [Agricultural Land Reform Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_Land_Reform_Code) (RA 3844) was a major Philippine [land reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_reform) law enacted in 1963 under President Macapagal.[^cite_note-442]

## Marcos era

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1965–1986)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1965%E2%80%931986\))



The participating leaders of the [Manila Summit Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Summit_Conference) in front of the Congress Building in [Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila,_Philippines), hosted by Philippine President [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) (4th from left) on October 24, 1966.

Macapagal ran for re-election in 1965, but was defeated by his former [party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Philippines\))-mate, Senate President [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos), who had switched to the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party). Early in his presidency, Marcos initiated public works projects and intensified tax collection.[^cite_note-\:10-443] In a failed attempt to retake east [Sabah](28678.md), the [Jabidah massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabidah_massacre), where Muslim Tausug Filipinos were killed by the Philippine Army, occurred under the authority of Marcos.[^cite_note-444] Due to his popularity among Christians, Marcos was re-elected president in 1969, becoming the first president of the Philippines to get a second term.[^cite_note-\:10-443] Crime and civil disobedience increased. The [Communist Party of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Philippines) formed the [New People's Army](1262096.md) and the [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md) continued to fight for an independent Muslim nation in Mindanao. An explosion which killed opposition lawmakers during the proclamation rally of the senatorial slate of the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Philippines\)) on August 21, 1971, led Marcos to suspend the [writ of habeas corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writ_of_habeas_corpus). Protests surged and the writ was restored on January 11, 1972.[^cite_note-auto-445]



The [Marcoses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) and the [Nixons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) at the [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace)

### Martial law

Main articles: [Martial law under Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_under_Ferdinand_Marcos) and [Human rights abuses of the Marcos dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_of_the_Marcos_dictatorship)

Amid the growing popularity of the opposition, Marcos declared [martial law](50477.md) on September 21, 1972, by virtue of [Proclamation No. 1081](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_No._1081) to stifle dissent. Marcos justified the declaration by citing the threat of Communist insurgency and the alleged ambush of defense secretary Juan Ponce Enrile.[^cite_note-auto-445] Ruling by decree, Marcos curtailed press freedom and other civil liberties, abolished Congress, closed down major media establishments, ordered the arrest of opposition leaders and militant activists, including his staunchest critics: senators [Benigno Aquino Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_Jr.), [Jovito R. Salonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jovito_R._Salonga), and [José W. Diokno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_W._Diokno).[^cite_note-uslc-28-446][^cite_note-auto-445] Crime rates plunged dramatically after a curfew was implemented.[^cite_note-447] Many protesters, students, and political opponents were forced to go into exile, and a number were killed.[^cite_note-auto-445][^cite_note-auto1-448]

A [constitutional convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_convention_\(political_meeting\)), which had been called for in 1970 to replace the colonial [1935 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1935_Constitution_of_the_Philippines), continued the work of framing a new constitution after the declaration of martial law. The new constitution went into effect in early 1973, changing the form of government from presidential to [parliamentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system) and allowing Marcos to stay in power beyond 1973. Marcos claimed that martial law was the prelude to creating a "New Society", which he would rule for more than two decades.[^cite_note-auto-445] The economy during the 1970s was robust, due to previous engagements by various administrations. However, the economy suffered after incurring massive debt and downgrading prospects of the Philippines under martial rule, while the wife of the president, [Imelda Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imelda_Marcos), lived in high society.[^cite_note-auto-445][^cite_note-auto1-448]

The [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_abuses_of_the_Marcos_dictatorship)[^cite_note-McCoy199909202-449][^cite_note-Abinales\&Amoroso20052-17] under the [dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) particularly targeted political opponents, student activists,[^cite_note-450] journalists, religious workers, farmers, and others who fought back against the administration. Based on the documentation of [Amnesty International](18947898.md), [Task Force Detainees of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_Force_Detainees_of_the_Philippines), and similar human rights monitoring entities,[^cite_note-RachelAGReyes201604122-451] the dictatorship was marked by 3,257 known extrajudicial killings,[^cite_note-RachelAGReyes201604122-451] 35,000 documented tortures, 77 'disappeared', and 70,000 incarcerations.[^cite_note-\:02-452][^cite_note-\:12-453]

Some 2,520 of the 3,257 murder victims were tortured and mutilated before their bodies were dumped in various places for the public to discover – a tactic meant to sow fear among the public,[^cite_note-\:02-452][^cite_note-\:22-454] which came to be known as "salvaging."[^cite_note-455] Some bodies were even cannibalized.[^cite_note-\:52-456]

### Fourth Republic



Manila circa 1980s

Marcos officially lifted martial law on January 17, 1981. However, he retained much of the government's power for arrest and detention. [Corruption](28460051.md) and [nepotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepotism) as well as civil unrest contributed to a serious decline in economic growth and development under Marcos, whose own health faced obstacles due to [lupus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lupus_erythematosus). The political opposition boycotted the [1981 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Philippine_general_election), which pitted Marcos against retired general [Alejo Santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejo_Santos), in protest over his control over the results.[^cite_note-uslc-28-446] Marcos won by a margin of over 16 million votes, allowing him to have another six-year term under the new Constitution that his administration crafted.[^cite_note-auto1-448] Finance Minister [Cesar Virata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesar_Virata) was eventually appointed to succeed Marcos as Prime Minister.[^cite_note-457]

In 1983, opposition leader [Ninoy Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino) was assassinated at [Manila International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino_International_Airport) upon his return to the Philippines after a long period of exile. This coalesced popular dissatisfaction with Marcos and began a succession of events, including pressure from the United States, that culminated in a snap [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Philippine_presidential_election) in February 1986.[^cite_note-auto1-448] The opposition united under Aquino's widow, [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino). The official election canvasser, the [Commission on Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Elections) (Comelec), declared Marcos the winner of the election. However, there was a large discrepancy between the Comelec results and that of [Namfrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namfrel), an accredited poll watcher. The allegedly fraudulent result was rejected by local and international observers.[^cite_note-uslc-29-458] [Cardinal Jaime Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaime_Sin) declared support for Corazon Aquino, which encouraged popular revolts.[^cite_note-459] General [Fidel Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Ramos) and Defense Minister [Juan Ponce Enrile](3322486.md) withdrew their support for Marcos. A peaceful civilian-military uprising, now popularly called the [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution), forced Marcos into exile and installed [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino) as president on February 25, 1986. The administration of Marcos has been called by various sources as a [kleptocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleptocracy)[^cite_note-460][^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462] and a [conjugal dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugal_dictatorship).[^cite_note-\:10-443][^cite_note-auto1-448]

## Fifth Republic (1986–present)

Main article: [History of the Philippines (1986–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1986%E2%80%93present\))

Further information: [1986–90 Philippine coup attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986%E2%80%9390_Philippine_coup_attempts)

### Administration of Corazon Aquino (1986–1992)

Main article: [Presidency of Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Corazon_Aquino)



[Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino), widow of the [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Ninoy_Aquino) opposition leader [Ninoy Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino), takes the Oath of Office on February 25, 1986



[Mount Pinatubo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Pinatubo) erupted in 1991.

Corazon Aquino immediately formed an [emergency government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_the_Philippines_\(1986%E2%80%931987\)) to try stabilizing the country's situation that provided for a transitional "[Freedom Constitution](19554336.md#The_1986_Freedom_Constitution)".[^cite_note-463] A new permanent constitution would later be ratified and enacted in February 1987.[^cite_note-464]

The constitution crippled presidential power to declare martial law, proposed the creation of autonomous regions in the [Cordilleras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cordillera_Administrative_Region) and [Muslim Mindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Region_in_Muslim_Mindanao), and restored the presidential form of government and the bicameral Congress.[^cite_note-usdos-465]

Progress was made in revitalizing democratic institutions and respect for civil liberties, but Aquino's administration was also viewed as weak and fractious, and a return to full political stability and economic development was hampered by several attempted coups staged by disaffected members of the Philippine military.[^cite_note-cnn-cory-466]

Economic growth was additionally hampered by a series of natural disasters, including the [1991 eruption of Mount Pinatubo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_eruption_of_Mount_Pinatubo) that left 700 dead and 200,000 homeless.[^cite_note-467]

During the Aquino presidency, Manila witnessed [six unsuccessful coup attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986%E2%80%9390_Philippine_coup_attempts), the [most serious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Philippine_coup_attempt) occurring in December 1989.[^cite_note-468]

In 1991, the Philippine Senate rejected a treaty that would have allowed a 10-year extension of the U.S. military bases in the country.[^cite_note-469] The United States turned over [Clark Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Air_Base) in [Pampanga](255602.md) to the government in November, and [Subic Bay Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subic_Bay_Naval_Base) in [Zambales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zambales) in December 1992, ending almost a century of U.S. military presence in the Philippines.[^cite_note-470]

### Administration of Fidel V. Ramos (1992–1998)

Main article: [Presidency of Fidel V. Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Fidel_V._Ramos)



Ramos inaugurated as president in 1992

In the [1992 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Philippine_general_election), Defense Secretary [Fidel V. Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_V._Ramos), endorsed by Aquino, won the presidency with just 23.6% of the vote in a field of seven candidates. Early in his administration, Ramos declared "national reconciliation" his highest priority and worked at building a coalition to overcome the divisiveness of the Aquino years.[^cite_note-usdos-465]

He legalized the [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Philippines) and laid the groundwork for talks with communist insurgents, Muslim separatists, and military rebels, attempting to convince them to cease their armed activities against the government. In June 1994, Ramos signed into law a general conditional [amnesty](18940624.md) covering all rebel groups, and Philippine military and police personnel accused of crimes committed while fighting the insurgents.[^cite_note-\:9-471]

In October 1995, the government signed an agreement bringing the military insurgency to an end. A peace agreement with the [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md) (MNLF), a major separatist group fighting for an independent homeland in [Mindanao](20546.md), was signed in 1996, ending the 24-year-old struggle.[^cite_note-\:9-471] However, an MNLF splinter group, the [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md), continued the armed struggle for an Islamic state.

Efforts by Ramos supporters to gain passage of an amendment that would allow him to run for a second term were met with large-scale protests, leading Ramos to declare he would not seek re-election.[^cite_note-asiaweek-472]

On his presidency the [death penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_the_Philippines) was revived in the light of the rape-slay case of UPLB students [Eileen Sarmienta and Allan Gomez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murders_of_Eileen_Sarmenta_and_Allan_Gomez) in 1993 and the first person to be executed was [Leo Echegaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Echegaray) in 1999.[^cite_note-473]

### Administration of Joseph Estrada (1998–2001)

Main article: [Presidency of Joseph Estrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Joseph_Estrada)



President Estrada in 2000

[Joseph Estrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Estrada), a former movie actor who had served as Ramos' vice president, was elected president by a landslide victory [in 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Philippine_presidential_election). His election campaign pledged to help the poor and develop the country's agricultural sector. He enjoyed widespread popularity, particularly among the poor.[^cite_note-bbc-erap-474] Estrada assumed office amid the [Asian Financial Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Financial_Crisis). The economy did, however, recover from a low −0.6% growth in 1998 to a moderate growth of 3.4% by 1999.[^cite_note-475]

Like his predecessor there was a similar attempt to change the 1987 constitution. The process is termed as CONCORD or Constitutional Correction for Development. Unlike Charter change under Ramos and Arroyo the CONCORD proposal, according to its proponents, would only amend the 'restrictive' economic provisions of the constitution that is considered as impeding the entry of more foreign investments in the Philippines. However, it was not successful in amending the constitution.[^cite_note-476]

After the worsening secessionist movement in Mindanao in April 2000, Estrada declared an "[all-out-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Philippine_campaign_against_the_Moro_Islamic_Liberation_Front)" against the [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md) (MILF).[^cite_note-Erap2008-09-18-477][^cite_note-478] The government later captured 46 MILF camps [including the MILF's headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Camp_Abubakar), [Camp Abubakar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Abubakar).[^cite_note-Erap2008-09-18-477][^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480]

In October 2000, however, Estrada was accused of having accepted millions of pesos in payoffs from illegal gambling businesses.[^cite_note-481] He was [impeached](15328.md) by the House of Representatives[^cite_note-482] but his impeachment trial in the Senate broke down when the senate voted to block examination of the president's bank records. In response, [massive street protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EDSA_Revolution_of_2001) erupted demanding Estrada's resignation. Faced with street protests, cabinet resignations, and a withdrawal of support from the armed forces, Estrada resigned from office on January 20, 2001.[^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484]

### Administration of Gloria Macapagal Arroyo (2001–2010)

Main article: [Presidency of Gloria Macapagal Arroyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Gloria_Macapagal_Arroyo)



President Arroyo between the monarchs of Spain in 2006

Vice President [Gloria Macapagal Arroyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Macapagal_Arroyo) (the daughter of President [Diosdado Macapagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Macapagal)) was sworn in as Estrada's successor on the day of his departure. Her accession to power was further legitimized by the mid-term congressional and local elections held four months later, when her coalition won an overwhelming victory.[^cite_note-uslc-pro-485]

Arroyo's initial term in office was marked by fractious coalition politics as well as a military mutiny in Manila in July 2003 that led her to declare a month-long nationwide state of rebellion.[^cite_note-uslc-pro-485] Later on in December 2002 she said would not run in the May 10, 2004, presidential election, but she reversed herself in October 2003 and decided to join the race anyway.[^cite_note-uslc-pro-485]

She was elected and sworn in for her own six-year term as president on June 30, 2004. In 2005, a tape of a wiretapped conversation surfaced bearing the voice of Arroyo apparently asking an election official if her margin of victory could be maintained.[^cite_note-cnn-trans-486] The tape sparked protests calling for Arroyo's resignation.[^cite_note-cnn-trans-486] Arroyo admitted to inappropriately speaking to an election official, but denied allegations of fraud and refused to step down.[^cite_note-cnn-trans-486] Attempts to impeach the president failed later that year.

Halfway through her second term, Arroyo unsuccessfully attempted to push for an overhaul of the constitution to transform the present presidential-bicameral republic into a federal parliamentary-unicameral form of government, which critics describe would be a move that would allow her to stay in power as Prime Minister.[^cite_note-487]

Her term saw the completion of infrastructure projects like [Line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_Light_Rail_Transit_System_Line_2) in 2004.[^cite_note-488]

Numerous other scandals (such as the [Maguindanao massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maguindanao_massacre), wherein 58 people were killed, and the unsuccessful [NBN-ZTE broadband deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBN%E2%80%93ZTE_deal_corruption_scandal)) took place in the dawn of her administration.

On May 20, 2008, the [K–12 curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%E2%80%9312_\(education\)) was implemented in the Philippines through the Omnibus Education Reform Act of 2008 filed by [Mar Roxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar_Roxas) and further emphasized by ASEAN Charter on December 15, 2008, adding two more years to the country's pre-university cycle. Its implementation process took 9 years and three presidents from May 2008 to June 5, 2017, from Arroyo to [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md).[^cite_note-489]

She formally ended her term as president on June 30, 2010 (wherein she was succeeded by Senator Benigno Aquino III) and ran for a seat in congress the same year (becoming the second president after Jose P. Laurel to run for lower office following the presidency).

### Administration of Benigno Aquino III (2010–2016)

Main article: [Presidency of Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Benigno_Aquino_III)

| icon | This section **may need to be rewritten to comply with Wikipedia's [quality standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Manual_of_Style)**, as paragraphs contains several short sentences; needs copyediting for better flow. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:History_of_the_Philippines#). [You can help](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=History_of_the_Philippines\&action=edit). The talk page may contain suggestions. *(May 2023)* |
| --- | --- |



President Aquino with U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) in 2011

[Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_III), the son of president [Corazon C. Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_C._Aquino), began his presidency on June 30, 2010. His administration claimed to be focused on major reforms that would bring greater transparency, reduced poverty, reduced corruption, and a booming market which will give birth to a newly industrialized nation.

He continued the 9-year implementation process of the K–12 curriculum that was started under his predecessor Gloria Macapagal Arroyo in 2008. Part of the it is the new curriculum's effectivity on April 24, 2012. To maintain continuity, Kindergarten was made compulsory through Kidergarten Education Reform Act of 2012 and the further 12 years by Enhanced Basic Education Act of 2013.[^cite_note-490][^cite_note-491]

The 2010 [Manila hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_hostage_crisis) caused deeply strained relations between [Manila](184334.md) and Hong Kong for a time.

The Sultanate of [Panay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panay), the newset of 21 in the country, was formally established covering 10 000 Muslims in the island.[^cite_note-492]

Tensions regarding [Sabah](28678.md) due to the [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)'s claim gradually rose during the early years of his administration. [Standoffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Lahad_Datu_standoff) in [Sabah](28678.md) between The [Sultanate of Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sulu)'s Royal Army and the Malaysian forces struck in 2013.[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494] A surprise incursion by the supporters of the Sultanate of Sulu on Sabah resulted in the killing of civilians and members of the Malaysian armed forces.[^cite_note-495] Following this attack, Malaysia ceased the annual cession of approximately \$1,000 to the Sultan's heirs, which it had honored as part of an 1878 [agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Borneo_dispute) with a British trading company over the use of the Sultanate's territory. In response to Malaysia's suspension of the annual payments, the Sulu claimants sought international arbitration, demanding US\$32 billion. In January 2022, Spanish arbitrator Gonzalo Stampa ruled in their favor, awarding a historic US\$14.9 billion. However, on 27 June 2023, the French Court of Appeals annulled the award, delivering a landmark victory for Malaysia.[^cite_note-496] Recently, Stampa was convicted of contempt of court for defying rulings from the Madrid High Court and sentenced to six months in prison.[^cite_note-497]

In 2012, the [Framework Agreement on the Bangsamoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framework_Agreement_on_the_Bangsamoro) was signed to create the Bangsamoro Government in [Mindanao](20546.md).[^cite_note-498] In response, the [Bangsamoro Islamic Freedom Fighters](39963003.md) (BIFF) was assembled by religious extremists with the goal of seceding from the Philippines.

The economy performed well at 7.2% GDP growth, the second fastest in Asia.



Aftermath of [Typhoon Haiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan) in [Tacloban](746499.md), [Leyte](194750.md)

The country was hit by [Typhoon Yolanda (Haiyan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan) on November 8, 2013, which heavily devastated the [Visayas](72418.md).[^cite_note-499][^cite_note-500] Massive rehabilitation efforts by foreign world powers sending aid, devolved into chaos following the revelations that the administration and that the government had not been properly handing out the aid packages and preference for political maneuvering over the safety of the people, leading to mass deterioration of food and medical supplies.

In 2014, the [Comprehensive Agreement on the Bangsamoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Agreement_on_the_Bangsamoro) was signed after 17 years of negotiation with the [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md) (MILF), a move which sought to bring peace in [Mindanao](20546.md) and the [Sulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulu).[^cite_note-501] When US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) visited the Philippines on April 28, 2014, Aquino signed the [Enhanced Defense Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_Defense_Cooperation_Agreement), facilitating the return of [United States Armed Forces](32212.md) bases into the country.[^cite_note-502][^cite_note-503][^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]

From January 15 to 19, 2015, [Pope Francis](1687680.md) [stayed in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Francis's_visit_to_the_Philippines) the Philippines for a series of publicity tours and paid visits to the victims of [Typhoon Haiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan_\(Yolanda\)).[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507] On January 25, 2015, 44 members of the [Philippine National Police](1313055.md)-[Special Action Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Action_Force) (PNP-SAF) were killed during [an encounter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamasapano_clash) between [MILF](51673.md) and [BIFF](39963003.md) in [Mamasapano, Maguindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamasapano,_Maguindanao), leading to a delay in the passage of the [Bangsamoro Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Basic_Law).[^cite_note-508][^cite_note-509]

Under Aquino's presidency, the Philippines has had controversial clashes with the People's Republic of China on a number of issues (such as the standoff in [Scarborough Shoal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarborough_Shoal) in the [South China Sea](74209.md) and the dispute over the [Spratly Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spratly_Islands)). This resulted in the proceedings of the Philippines to file a sovereignty case against China in a global arbitration tribunal. Later on in 2014, the Aquino Administration then [filed a case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_v._China) to the Arbitration Tribunal in [The Hague](30269.md) which challenged Beijing's claim in the [South China Sea](74209.md) after Chinese ships were accused of harassing a small Philippine vessel carrying goods for stationed military personnel in the [South Thomas Shoal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Thomas_Shoal?action=edit\&redlink=1) where an old Philippine ship had been stationed for many years.[^cite_note-510]

On January 12, 2016, the [Philippine Supreme Court](1335534.md) upheld the [Enhanced Defense Cooperation Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_Defense_Cooperation_Agreement) paving the way for the return of [United States Armed Forces](32212.md) bases into the country.[^cite_note-511] On March 23, 2016, [Diwata-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diwata-1) was launched to the [International Space Station](15043.md) (ISS), becoming the country's first micro-satellite and the first satellite to be built and designed by Filipinos.[^cite_note-512]

### Administration of Rodrigo Duterte (2016–2022)

Main article: [Presidency of Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Rodrigo_Duterte)



[Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md) delivering his first State of the Nation Address.

[Davao City](489401.md) mayor [Rodrigo Duterte](7407293.md) [succeeded Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election) and became the first president from [Mindanao](20546.md). Under [Duterte's first 100 days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_Rodrigo_Duterte's_presidency), the government launched a 24-hour complaint office accessible to the public through a nationwide hotline, [8888](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8888_\(Philippines\)), and changed the nationwide [emergency telephone number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_telephone_number) from 117 to [911](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9-1-1_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514] Duterte launched an [intensified anti-drug campaign](51032591.md) to fulfill a campaign promise of wiping out criminality in six months;[^cite_note-515] by August 2019, the death toll for the war on drugs was 5,779.[^cite_note-516][^cite_note-pnareal-517][^cite_note-CayetanoWarOnDrugsUPR-518]

The implementation of K–12 from 2008, carried over from his predecessors Arroyo and Aquino III, continued throughout Duterte administration until the new curriculum was implemented on Grade 6 on June 5, 2017, which entirely phased out the older 1945–2017 K–4th Year system first used on May 28, 1945, and completed the 9-year process of the former.

On July 12, 2016, the [Permanent Court of Arbitration](38058.md) ruled in favor of the Philippines in its [case against China's claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_v._China) in the South China Sea; the ruling deprived surface features in the contested area of territorial-generating status, effectively deflating China's [nine-dash line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nine-dash_line) territorial claims.[^cite_note-theguardian2016-519] Later that November, president [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos)' remains were buried at the *[Libingan ng mga Bayani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libingan_ng_mga_Bayani)* (the country's official cemetery for heroes) after the [Supreme Court of the Philippines](1335534.md) ruled in favor of the burial, provoking protests from various groups.[^cite_note-520]

Following [clashes between government forces and the Maute group in Marawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marawi_crisis), Duterte, on May 23, 2017, signed [Proclamation No. 216](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_No._216) declaring a 60-day [martial law](50477.md) in Mindanao.[^cite_note-521] To attain inclusive economic growth and improve quality of life in the country, in 2017, the Duterte administration launched its socioeconomic policy, [DuterteNomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuterteNomics), in which infrastructure development and industrialization were a significant part of.[^cite_note-522] The policy included the [Build! Build! Build! Infrastructure Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build!_Build!_Build!_Infrastructure_Plan), which aimed to sustain the country's economic growth and accelerate poverty reduction[^cite_note-523] by developing transport infrastructure such as railways, roads, airports, and seaports, irrigation, and flood control projects.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-\:1-525] Duterte signed the [Universal Access to Quality Tertiary Education Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Access_to_Quality_Tertiary_Education_Act), providing free tuition and exemption from other fees in public universities and colleges, as well as subsidies for those enrolled in private higher education institutions. Duterte also signed into law the Universal Health Care Act, the creation of the [Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Human_Settlements_and_Urban_Development), establishing a national cancer control program, and allowing subscribers to keep their mobile numbers for life.[^cite_note-526]

In 2018, the [Bangsamoro Organic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Organic_Law) was legislated into law[^cite_note-willitlast-527] and was ratified following [a successful plebiscite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Bangsamoro_autonomy_plebiscite) a year later. The [Bangsamoro transition period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_transition_period) began, paving the way for the formal creation of the [Bangsamoro ARMM](37260549.md).[^cite_note-CarolynArguillas20190126-528]

### Administration of Bongbong Marcos (2022–present)

Main article: [Presidency of Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bongbong_Marcos)



Marcos delivers his [first State of the Nation Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_State_of_the_Nation_Address_\(Philippines\)) on July 25, 2022, with Senate President [Migz Zubiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migz_Zubiri) (left) and House Speaker [Martin Romualdez](45505193.md).

In May 2022, [Ferdinand Marcos Jr.](572460.md) (known by his nickname "Bongbong"), son of former president and dictator Ferdinand Marcos, won the [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_presidential_election) by a landslide. His vice presidential candidate was [Sara Duterte](42504861.md), daughter of then-president Rodrigo Duterte.[^cite_note-529] On June 30, 2022, Marcos was sworn in as the Philippine president and Sara Duterte was sworn in as vice-president.[^cite_note-530] A few weeks after his inauguration as president, the [2022 Luzon earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Luzon_earthquake) hit Northern Luzon, resulting in 11 casualties and 615 people injured.[^cite_note-531]

[Bagong Pilipinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagong_Pilipinas_\(campaign\)) (transl. New Philippines), the administration's term for its brand of governance and leadership, was officially launched with a [kick-off rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kickoff_meeting) on January 28, 2024, at the [Quirino Grandstand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quirino_Grandstand) in [Manila](184334.md), the capital of the Philippines. It was attended by about 400,000 supporters with the participation of [Vice President](2271639.md) [Sara Duterte](42504861.md), other key [Malacañang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) officials, and [Manila Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Manila) [Honey Lacuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honey_Lacuna).[^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533] The rally was streamed online to engage a wider audience beyond Manila.[^cite_note-534]

As president, Marcos signed into law the creation of the [Maharlika Investment Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maharlika_Investment_Fund), the first [sovereign wealth fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_wealth_fund) of the Philippines.[^cite_note-535] Under his term, the Philippines ratified the [RCEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Comprehensive_Economic_Partnership) in February, and entered into force in June 2023.[^cite_note-536][^cite_note-537] Marcos also went on many foreign trips in hopes to attract more foreign investments in the country.

During Marcos' administration, the number of [American citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans_in_the_Philippines) who lived in the country increased from 300,000 as of 2023,[^cite_note-538] to 750,000 by year 2025,[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-539] (forming 0.75% of the demographics)[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-539] mainly due to his liberal [visa policies](330265.md). Furthermore, up to 250,000 [Amerasians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerasian),[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-540] (0.25% of the country), can be found living across the cities of [Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Global_City), [Angeles](842728.md), Manila, and [Olongapo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olongapo)[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-540]\(When combined, [American](19792942.md)[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-539] and [Amerasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amerasian) descent,[^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-540] form 1% of the population).[^cite_note-ManilaBulletin-539][^cite_note-AmerasianResearch-540] Under Marcos' term despite the pressure of the US-Israel vs Iran war, the 2026 ASEAN summit held in the [Philippines](23440.md), had pushed through, and in the Philippines, East Timor's Catholic ally, was the first ASEAN summit wherein East Timor participated as a full member of ASEAN.[^cite_note-541] This occurred simultaneously with the chairmanship of [António Guterres](471806.md) who is the reigning [Secretary-General](162415.md) of the [United Nations](31769.md) and is of Portuguese and East Timorese ancestry.[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543]

Given the significance of the size and distribution of the population to the spread of diseases and their ability to become endemic, it is worth commenting briefly on the physical and human geography of the Philippines. The hot and humid tropical climate would have generally favored the propagation of many diseases, especially water-borne infections, though there might be regional or seasonal variations in climate that might affect the incidence of some diseases. In general, however, the fact that the Philippines comprise some seven thousand islands, some of which are uninhabited even today, would have discouraged the spread of infections, as would the low population density.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERausa-Gomez1967Lourdes_Rausa-Gomez_cited_Sir_Stamford_Raffles,_himself_citing_the_'Traditional_History_of_Java'_wherein_he_said_that_Manila_and_Sulu_in_the_Philippines_were_part_of_Majapahapit,_however_she_doubted_the_veracity_of_Stamford_Raffles_assertion_due_to_the_lack_of_archaeological_evidence_between_Majapahit_and_the_Philippines_in_her_1967_article._However,_that_article_has_been_renderred_outdated_due_to_the_discovery_of_the_Laguna_Copperplate_Inscription_in_1989_which_proved_links_between_Java_and_Manila,_which_makes_her_dismissal_of_the_Raffles_assertion_null_and_the_Raffles_assertion_feasible-135]: &#x20;[Rausa-Gomez 1967](#CITEREFRausa-Gomez1967), Lourdes Rausa-Gomez cited Sir Stamford Raffles, himself citing the 'Traditional History of Java' wherein he said that Manila and Sulu in the Philippines were part of Majapahapit, however she doubted the veracity of Stamford Raffles assertion due to the lack of archaeological evidence between Majapahit and the Philippines in her 1967 article. However, that article has been renderred outdated due to the discovery of the Laguna Copperplate Inscription in 1989 which proved links between Java and Manila, which makes her dismissal of the Raffles assertion null and the Raffles assertion feasible.
